===PAGE 2=== (ocr, 34 lines)
   1|                                                                       INMEMORIAM
   2|                                                              PAUL B. PORTER - Paul was born in Fitchburg and grew up in Lunenburg and
   3|                                                              graduated from Lunenburg High School. Following his graduation, Paul joined the U.S.
   4|                                                              Marine Corps and served for four years. He was honorably discharged in 1963 having
   5|                                                              attained the rank of corporal. When he returned home he became a reserve police officer
   6|                                                              with the Lunenburg Police Department and served in that capacity for 46 years.
   7|                                                              Paul was a member of the Lunenburg Planning Board and Zoning Board of Appeals. He
   8|                                                              was president of the Lunenburg Historical Society, member of the Masons and served as a
   9|                                                              Constable. He was member and past president of the Lunenburg Police Relief Association
  10|                                                              and senior vice commander of the American Legion Post 283.
  11|                           Paul was a proud Marine Corps Devil Dog and member and past Commandant of the Marine Corps League Fitchburg
  12|                           Detachment #1076. He was honored by the Department of Massachusetts Marine Corps League as their "Marine of the
  13|                           Year" for 2011-2012.
  14|                           He was affectionately referred to as "The Mayor of Lunenburg" by his daughter Pam and many others. Everyone who
  15|                           knew him loved him.
  16|                                                               RICHARD "NICK" STORES - Nick was born and brought up in New
  17|                                                               York, educated at Pomfret School and Yale University, and moved to Fitchburg in 1962 to
  18|                                                               teach    at Applewild School after teaching in Switzerland for two years. He was
  19|                                                               instrumental in forming the Fitchburg Youth Hockey League which played at Applewild's
  20|                                                               outdoor rink before the Wallace Civic Center was built in the early 1970s.
  21|                                                               Nick moved to Lancaster Avenue in Lunenburg in 1970 and quickly became involved with
  22|                                                               town activities. Whether it was sports or additional education programs he was always
  23|                                                                ready to volunteer for something. He introduced the Math Olympiad program to the 5th
  24|                                                                and 6th grades in the early 1980s and taught the kids the importance of thinking through a
  25|                                                                word problem rather than just concentrating on the "right" answer. Nick served on the
  26|                            School Committee for several years and became the Chairman in 1988. Always concerned about the children of
  27|                           Lunenburg getting the best education available, he worked closely with the Superintendents and other members of the
  28|                            School Committee toward that goal.
  29|                           In 1988 Nick became a Realtor with Century 21 Fontaine in Fitchburg and subsequently bought the franchise. He and
  30|                            his wife, Jeanie, created Century 21 Realty Team and moved the business to Leominster. Covering most of the towns
  31|                            of Northern Worcester County, the philosophy of the company has always focused on service and serving the real
  32|                            estate needs of the community. As a very active member of the Montachusett Area Rotary Club Nick's enthusiasm for
  33|                           "Service Above Self" extended to both local civic projects as well as multiple trips to Central America with Habitat for
  34|                            Humanity. At home, he became a Commissioner of Trust Funds and served as Chairman until his death in 2012

===PAGE 9=== (ocr, 43 lines)
   1|                                                                      REPORT OF THE TOWN MANAGER
   2|                    The Town continued to make important progress in 2012 in many areas, including: School Building; Renewable Energy;
   3|                  . Roadway Improvements and Building Preservation. In addition, we hit a milestone by surpassing 10,000 in population.
   4|                    In 2012, we bid farewell to both Police Chief Dan Bourgeois and Fire Chief Scott Glenny. Together, these two fine
   5|                    gentlemen provided a combined 62 years of service to the Town of Lunenburg! They worked diligently up until the end,
   6|                    particularly in finalizing the Town's plan to join the Nashoba Valley Regional Emergency Communication Center. In 2012,
   7|                    we also welcomed long-time Police Lieutenant Jim Marino as our new Police Chief, and long-time Deputy Fire Chief Pat
   8|                    Sullivan as our new Fire Chief.
   9|                    In 2012, the Town formed a School Building Committee to continue working toward the goal of building a new or renovate
  10|                    an existing school building. Please review the Superintendent of School's report for additional information.
  11|                    A good portion of the first half of 2012 was dedicated to reviewing the FY2013 Budget proposal and Override request. After
  12|                    a significant amount of discussion, the Board of Selectmen, School Committee and Finance Committee agreed that it was
  13|                    time to ask the community to consider an Override. The impact of the $2.2M Operating Override to the Average
  14|                    Homeowner was determined to be just under $500 per year. In the end, the Override was defeated by a large margin, and
  15|                    as a result, several large scale changes were made, including the decision to consolidate four schools into three and to
  16|                    transition all Administrative and Land Use Offices to a 4-day work schedule.
  17|                    A significant amount of time was spent this past year reviewing three different, large solar projects: Borrego at the Town's
  18|                    Landfill; EPG at Pleasant & West Streets; and Mass PV1 on Chase Road. The project at the Town's Landfill site is a town-
  19|                    sponsored project, while the other two are private projects. Unfortunately, the project at the Landfill was determined to be
  20|                    uneconomic. The two private projects are still viable. Both private projects will provide the Town with the opportunity to
  21|                    enter into Power Purchase Agreements, whereby the Town can purchase Net Metering Credits as a discounted price to be
  22|                    used to offset the Town's electric utility costs for municipal buildings.
  23|                    This year, we saw the sale of a town-owned historic building to a private party. The Jones House- the former homestead of
  24|                    American botanist, horticulturalist and pioneer in agricultural science Luther Burbank, was sold in June to Larry & Dawn
  25|                    Marshall. Under the conditions of sale, Mr. & Mrs. Marshall are required to restore the property in keeping with its historical
  26|                    significance. The property's restoration will be monitored by the Town's Historical Commission. To date, the Marshall's
  27|                    have done an excellent job!
  28|                    In June, with the assistance of the Town's Treasurer-Collector, we were able to refund/ refinance our existing 2003 Bond
  29|                    Issue, which resulted in a net savings to the Town of $550,000 over the 11 remaining years of the issue. In the process, the
  30|                    Town's AA- Bond Rating was affirmed.
  31|                    Finally, the Town continues to push forward in the quest to redevelop the Summer Street Corridor. In 2012, Town Meeting
  32|                    authorized funding for an Alternatives Analysis. This was completed and the Board of Selectmen agreed upon a redesign
  33|                  : which includes one travel lane in each direction, with center turn lanes as specific intersections. The next step in the
  34|                      rocess includes bringing the project to 25% design, at which point, it can be included in the region's TIP (Transportation
  35|                      mprovement Plan), which will secure grant funding for construction. The Town owes a big thank you to its legislativ
  36|                     delegation- Senator Flanagan, Representative Benson and Representative DiNatale- who were all instrumental in securing
  37|                     he remaining funding for design in the State's Transportation Bond Bill. As a result of Bond Bill and TIP funding, about
  38|                    95% of this project's funding will come from sources other than the Lunenburg taxpayer.
  39|                    I would be remiss if I did not take this opportunity to thank all of the people in this town who give of their time and many
  40|                    talents to make Lunenburg a better place. Thank you all for your dedicated service.
  41|                    Respectfully submitted,
  42|                    Kerry A. Speidel
  43|                                                                                             6

   1|                                                                               BUILDING DEPARTMENT
   2|                       The Building Department is located on the second floor of the Ritter Memorial Building, 960 Massachusetts Avenue,
   3|                       Lunenburg, MA. Office hours for the Building Department are Monday and Wednesdays 8:00 A.M. to 4:00 P.M., Tuesday
   4|                       and Thursday 8:00 A.M. until 6:00 P.M., and closed on Fridays. Michael J. Sauvageau serves as the Building
   5|                       Commissioner, James Maillet, Assistant Inspector and Lisa Normandin, Administrative Assistant. Appointments with the
   6|                       Building Inspector are recommended but not required.
   7|                       Building permits remain steady for additions, remodeling, roofing, siding, window replacement and decks. Several solar
   8|                    •panel installations for both residential and commercial buildings were permitted and increasing each year. Apartment
   9|                     • construction located at Tri Town Landing is moving forward with building number three under construction consisting of
  10|                       thirty-three apartments. The Emerald Place development at the former Whalom Park is moving ahead steadily with all 238
  11|                       building permits issued.       A record number of wood and pellet stove permits were installed and inspected. The number of
  12|                       building permits issued in 2012 was 290.
  13|                       The Commonwealth of Massachusetts has adopted the 2009 International Residential Code and 2009 International Energy
  14|                       Conservation Code. The 2009 International Building Code is currently in effect, this is the 8h Edition of the Massachusetts
  15|                       Code with amendments. The new code affects commercial and residential buildings and additions. All contractors are
  16|                       encouraged to review the new compliance regulations of the code.
  17|                       Carbon monoxide detectors are required in every home existing as well as new construction. Homeowners should be
  18|                       aware that inflatable swimming pools do not meet code compliance, please contact our office to determine what needs to
  19|                       be done for compliance and safety.
  20|                       Residents should be aware that Massachusetts Law requires that all persons, partnerships and corporations who bid or
  21|                       perform residential contracting (reconstruction, alterations, additions etc.) for work exceeding $1000.00 be registered with
  22|                       the Commonwealth. This office will enforce this provision; however you should ask your contractor about his/her
  23|                       registration. Please contact this office with any questions about this requirement or call for any information regarding
  24|                       zoning requirements or building code issues.
  25|                                                                        CAPITAL PLANNING TOWN REPORT
  26|                       Lunenburg's Charter specifically mandates that a Capital Planning Committee prepare a Fiscal Year (FY) Plan and a Capital
  27|                       Improvement Plan. These Plans are to be presented to the Town Manager.
  28|                       As noted in the past reports the criterion for any capital request includes Mandates, Safety, Need,                               Crisis, Cost
  29|                       Effectiveness and Ability to Fund. The Committee has to review each request under these six elements. Not only do they
  30|                      have to determine that the request fits within the criteria, but they have to prioritize the level that meets the criteria.
  31|                       Therefore, not all requests are included in the FY Plans, mostly due to funding. The result of the proposed FY Plans
  32|                       provides the needed service for safety and protection of the public, the upkeep to our schools and the management of
  33|                       government.
  34|                       At the Annual Town Meeting May 2012, $464,487.00 was approved for Capital Requests.
  35|                       The following items were presented in the Plan.
  36|                       DEPARTMENT                                      ITEM                                            AMOUNT

   6|                                                                               PLANNING BOARD
   7|                   The Planning Board accepted the resignation of Robert Saila on December 31, 2012. The Board wishes Bob good luck with his many projects.
   8|                   The Planning Board, aware of the economy as in their monitoring in 2011, continued to monitor its effect on Lunenburg's
   9|                   housing situation in 2012. The office continues receiving many requests for information from the citizenry via personal concerns about housing status.
  10|                The number of AN plans (subdivision of lots for separate building lots) increased to 11 for 2012 from the previous year of
  11|                   five (5). The major large project, Emerald Place at Lake Whalom, continued construction according to the approved plan
  12|                  with three different housing styles. They are constructed villas with two (2) units (single floor design) per structure. The
  13|                   developer's plans are to construct to completion. The units are occupied as each unit or set of units are completed,
  14|                   contracted under "lease to own" agreements.
  15|                  Tri Town Landing is under construction for Building Three (3). The 40R project Building One & Two are occupied as rentals
  16|                  and all sixty-six (66) units have been rented. Building Three (3) will be rentals as well. The Town has received compliments
  17|                  on the construction and management of this project.
  18|                  Stone Farm, Massachusetts Avenue is still being constructed to completion. Highfield Village, Northfield Road is still in
  19|                  review. Several extensions have been given. The project entitled Whitetail Crossing has submitted its "As Built" and plans
  20|                  request. on requesting Town acceptance of the roadway interior. Sequoia Drive and Whites Woods Phase Ill are building upon
  21|                  The Open Space Plan, under the auspices of the Planning Board and Conservation Commission, was reported previously as approved by the State.
  22|                                                   The Ad Hoc Committee for that Plan continues to work, as recommended, to address
  23|                  implementation. In 2011 the Committee started to look into an important element in our community; the farming
  24|                  community. They began researching the possibility of recommending an Agricultural Commission and the creation of a
  25|                  "Right to Farm" bylaw. This work continued in 2012 and will involve the farming community. Many farmers have shown
  26|                  interest. They are working with a State Consultant.
  27|                  Work began in 2012 to update the Master Plan and Zoning Bylaw. In addition to the two Zoning Bylaws approved at the
  28|                  December 2011 Special Town Meeting; 1) Design Standards Bylaw, and, 2) Summer Street Revitalization Overlay District
  29|                  Bylaw, both of which have been approved by the Attorney General's office, the Board has continued working with MRPC
  30|                  and the local Board of Selectmen regarding the revitalization of Summer Street. This project will be a complete redesign of Summer Street.
  31|                 In addition, the Planning Office worked with MRPC on a DLTA Grant (District Local Technical Assistance) which content
  32|                 includes data on employment, population characteristics, economic sector/local tax base, education, brownfields, Chapter
  33|                  43D sites, transportation, and environmental and development characteristics of land by zoning. All the above data are the
  34|                  background for updating the Master Plan. A public presentation was held for the Town's information. The Board plans on
  35|                  continuing work with MRPC in this direction and will be able to apply for the next round. The two issues noted by the
  36|                  Board are economics and center planning.
  37|                  In 2012 the major planning issue was of renewable energy. The Planning Board worked on permitting three solar projects.
  38|                 The three projects were the Town's landfill, 651 Chase Road and 134 Electric Avenue. The projects also went under a
  39|                 Development Plan Review. These applications prompted the Planning Board to review the Town's 2009 solar bylaw. An
  40|                 updated bylaw was written and passed at the May 2012 Town Meeting. As of this report, the projects are in discussion with
  41|                 the Board of Selectmen concerning the method of payment; taxes versus a PILOT program.
  42|                 Thomas Bodkin Jr., Clerk, remains the Planning Board representative to the Montachusetts Regional Planning
  43|                 Commission. Joanna Bilotta-Simeone replaced Robert Salia as the representative to the Montachusetts Joint 10

   7|                                                                               BOARD OF HEALTH
   8|                      As a reminder, the Board of Health office has been relocated to the old Ritter Library building on the corner of Mass. Ave
   9|                      and Leominster Road. Our office is on the upper level at the end of the hall on the right. We can be reached at 978-345-
  10|                       4146, extension 430.
  11|                      The Board of Health, with the assistance of its Agent, the Nashoba Associated Boards of Health, continued its mission to
  12|                      maintain my maintain its focus on public health and safety issues during 2012, reviewing Title V Inspection Reports,
  13|                      building permits, septic and well permits, nuisance investigations, housing inspections, complaints and general public
  14|                      health related issues.
  15|                      Our partnership with the Central Massachusetts Mosquito Control Project again proved valuable to the Town. Numerous
  16|                      scheduled mosquito sprayings were conducted during the warmer weather period to control the mosquito population
  17|                      within the town. Hundreds of citizens also requested sprayings of their yards. The CMMCP stations mosquito traps for
  18|                      testing around the town and twice discovered insects infected with EEE virus. Sprayings were conducted in those areas as
  19|                      well as a Special spraying of all the school grounds just prior to the Bonfire.
  20|                      Citizens who wish to have their property sprayed for mosquitos should call the Board of Health office who will add you to
  21|                      the schedule. There is no charge for this service.
  22|                      Homeland Security planning is ongoing. Meetings continue between our agents, other Town Departments and regional
  23|                      entities to develop a plan for not only Lunenburg but with our partner towns in our region.
  24|                      This year, we processed applications for Septic Permit Lot Tests, permits for new construction, new wells, repairs or
  25|                      upgrades to septic systems and well permits. The Board continues its vigilance with regard to septic systems, wells, food
  26|                      service inspections, housing and nuisance complaints and its general mandate to protect the environment and
  27|                      groundwaters of the Commonwealth.
  28|                      The Board of Health provides numerous other services in cooperation with our Health Agents, the Nashoba Associated
  29|                      Boards of Health. In addition to our environmental services, numerous clinics are provided throughout the year to our
  30|                     residents. These clinics provide inoculations, Blood Pressure screenings, Well Oldster Clinics and Dental screening.
  31|                      Fluoride treatment and dental checks of our youngsters in the School System are provided by the Nashoba dental staff.
  32|                      With our Nashoba Nursing and Hospice partners we provide in home visits to ill residents recovering from illness and
  33|                      Hospice care and counseling to those in need of this service.
  34|                      Please contact our office in the Ritter Building for more information.
  35|                     Please remember that our Board Members volunteer their time and efforts on behalf of you, the Citizens of Lunenburg. If
  36|                     you have something that you think we can help you with, we are at your service. Contact the Board office at 582-4146 ext.
  37|                     430.

  13|                    Environmental Health Department
  14|                                          Environmental Information Responses
  15|                              Lunenburg Office (days)..                                ...........88
  16|                               The Nashoba sanitarian is generally scheduled to be available for the public twice a week on Monday and Wednesday mornings at the Lunenburg Board of Health Office. Other meetings occur informally.
  17|                               This does not reflect the daily calls handled by the three Nashoba secretaries during daily business hours.
  18|                               Food Service Licenses & Inspections.......                     ...31
  19|                                         Nashoba annually mails out and receives application from both restaurants and retail food businesses. Licenses
  20|                                         are renewed for existing facilities. Plans are submitted and reviewed prior to initial licensing. Most licensees are
  21|                                        inspected at a minimum twice a year. Where deficiencies are found, a re-inspection is scheduled to insure
  22|                                        compliance. When a complaint from the public is received an inspection is also conducted. During this
  23|                                        inspection health factors of food handlers is also investigated, and where appropriate medical consultation and laboratory testing may be required.
  24|                              Beach/Camp Inspections...                                    .... 39
  25|                                        Camps are inspected at least annually at opening for compliance with State Sanitary Code, Chapter IV,
  26|                                        105CMR430.00. Public swimming beaches are sampled for water quality every week during the summer and more often if a problem is suspected.

  10|                                                                              HISTORICAL COMMISSION
  11|                       The Lunenburg Historical Commission was created to identify, safeguard and help preserve for posterity, the unique
  12|                      physical assets of the Town as exemplified by the various sites, buildings and other edifices of historic, architectural or archeological significance to the Town of Lunenburg
  13|                      The Mission Statement above has served to be the guiding principle for the Historical Commission to focus its efforts and
  14|                      follows: work on a number of projects of historical consequence for the Town. The main initiatives implemented this year are as
  15|                      Jones House - The successful conclusion to the Commission's efforts to sell the Jones House at 42 Main Street as a
  16|                      historic property were realized. Mr. & Mrs. Lawrence Matthews purchased the house from the Town and embarked on the
  17|                      process of restoring this significant historic property.
  18|                      Lunenburg Ledger - The program of regular newspaper articles was continued by the Commission to inform Town.
  19|                      residents of the unique historical qualities of life in Lunenburg both past and present.
  20|                      Capital Plan - The Commission was consulted this year and we urged that efforts be made in the funding of the Capital
  21|                      Plan to include new paint for the Town Hall along with repairs to the chimneys and the roof.
  22|                      Funding Grant - The Commission sought and was given a $3,000. grant by the Montachusett Society for the Preservation of Historic Buildings to be used for the chimney work at the Town Hall.
  23|                      Demolition Delay Requests - The Commission continued to work closely with the Building Commissioner to review and
  24|                      determine the appropriate course of action for properties seeking demolition permits. During the year multiple properties
  25|                      were reviewed and acted upon. The most significant involved a historic property on Leominster-Shirley Road. The
  26|                      Commission has made efforts to make citizens aware of the structure on the property in the hope that it might be relocated.
  27|                      Computerized Database - The development of the Town wide computerized database utilizing the Massachusetts Historical
  28|                      Commission website is an ongoing project. The completion of the web based resource continues to be a main focus of the
  29|                      Commission's long term goals.
  30|                     The Commission meetings are scheduled the first Tuesday of each month at 6:30PM at The Ritter Building except during
  31|                    the months of July & August when meetings are on an as needed basis.
  32|                     Respectfully submitted,
  33|                     Patrick J. Slattery, Chairman                         Amory Phelps, Member                       Damon McQuaid, Secretary
  34|                     Rebecca Lantry, Vice Chairman                         Richard McGrath, Member

  22|                                                                              FINANCE COMMITTEE
  23|                   2012 represented a crossroads for the Town of Lunenburg. With decreases in state aid and increases in costs that continue to outpace increases in revenues, we were faced with a very difficult decision: Accept the reduced revenues and
  24|                   thereby begin dismantling key services provided by the Town or increase revenues in the form of a Proposition 2 ½ override and maintain level services. The Town chose the former by a 2-1 margin at the polls.
  25|                   into balance. The balanced budget for FY2013 as approved at Annual Town Meeting required that we limit spending in all areas of town government. With limited ability to increase revenues, we were forced to make dramatic cuts in costs to bring the budget
  26|                   In recent years we had taken extraordinary steps to balance the budget, such as furloughs, layoffs, drawing from stabilization to fund unemployment and short term borrowing to fund capital. We do not see this as sustainable. The
  27|               • balanced budget put into effect resulted in layoffs, which resulted in costs to fund unemployment. However, we did not draw from stabilization nor did we borrow for capital articles this year because we need to protect our bond rating in
  28|                  spite of our good intentions. anticipation of a future school building project. Note that some of the proposed cuts could jeopardize our bond rating in
  29|                  As reported in the last several budget letters from this committee, we have been struggling with a structural deficit for 16

===PAGE 20=== (ocr, 53 lines)
   1|                   some time now. The reality is that costs and expenditures that we do not control are growing faster than our ability to
   2|                   increase revenues. This has been going on for a number of years and is made worse by continued reductions in state aid.
   3|                   On the cost side, we continue to see increases in health care costs, albeit not as much as in prior years. To help offset
   4|                   this, we have shifted a greater share of these costs to employees, with 75% now paid by the Town and 25% now paid by
   5|                   employees. Unemployment expense due to layoffs and liability insurance expense due to Public Safety Injured on Duty
   6|                   claims are up considerably. Other pressures on costs include increases in our Monty Tech assessment due to increased
   7|                   enrollment and increases in special education tuitions and student transportation. The debt service is increased primarily
   8|                   due to the borrowing to fund capital articles in FY2011 and FY2012 and permanent financing on the DPW facility. Other
   9|                   pressures on costs include contractual increases in wages, although some employees have agreed to no cost of living
  10|                   increases.
  11|                   We have four sources of revenue to the Town: property tax, state aid, local receipts and available funds. Federal stimulus
  12|                   monies which provided temporary additional revenues are no longer available. Increases in property tax are limited by
  13|                   Proposition 2 ½: We are not allowed to increase the tax levy by more than 2.5% unless we approve an override. New
  14|                   growth is expected to contribute a $350K increase to property tax revenues; however, please note that growth comes with additional strain on services and infrastructure. The additional tax we gain from new growth is not enough to offset the
  15|                   increase in demand for town services like public safety and education, as well as additional traffic on our roads. State aid
  16|                   in the form of Chapter 70 funding for education has increased slightly; however, other forms of state aid have been
  17|                  reduced. Local receipts are in decline, with increases in motor vehicle excise tax being offset by decreases in
  18|                   supplemental tax and supplemental state aid. Note that state lottery aid has declined by $486K since 2009, or 37%.
  19|                   The FY2013 balanced budget funds level service in public safety and painful cuts on other services, such as schools,
  20|                    Library, Council on Aging and town offices. To meet the $1.2 million shortfall in education, the school district decided to
  21|                   consolidate from four schools into three, moving students out of TC Passios and restructuring the grade configuration at
  22|                   the Primary School, Turkey Hill Middle School and Lunenburg High School. Despite this restructuring the School District
  23|                   was still forced to eliminate additional teaching positions on top of the 18.5 positions that were eliminated between 2009
  24|                   and 2012, resulting in larger class sizes.
  25|                    The Council on Aging was faced with a 10% reduction that led to reduced operating hours. At the Library, we were faced
  26|                   with a similar dilemma until special Town Meeting last fall, where the Town approved a supplemental appropriation to bring
  27|                  the Library budget in line with State guidelines and preserve our eligibility for matching funds and inter-library services
  28|                   without the need for a waiver. Note that this appropriation was approved against the recommendations of the Select Board
  29|                    and the Finance Committee, who were concerned that additional state funding used for this appropriation could not be
  30|                    depended upon. State aid was indeed subsequently reduced.
  31|                    Town offices experienced layoffs and services were reduced to a four-day schedule. Again we were not able to provide
  32|                    funding for the Comprehensive Pavement Management Plan, meaning that we cannot address proper maintenance of our
  33|                    roads. The condition of our Town roadways will continue to deteriorate as a result. The Capital Planning Committee
  34|                    recommendation for purchases in Technology, School Infrastructure, Public Works, Public Safety and Council on Aging
  35|                    was approved and funded without borrowing.
  36|                    To summarize, the impact of a balanced budget and failure of the proposed Proposition 21/2 override is real and being felt
  37|                    virtually every day. The Town made their choice clear by a two to one margin at the annual Town election. The Town
  38|                   Manager and Select Board, with the support of the Finance Committee and all the department heads, continue to make the
  39|                    challenging decisions to honor the wishes of the Town's people and manage through this difficult time.
  40|                    In closing, the Finance Committee wishes to thank all of the dedicated public servants for their hard work and diligence.
  41|                 Mark Erickson, Chair                          Scott Gile
  42|                    Brian Laffond, Vice Chair                   John Male
  43|                    Terri Burchfield, Secretary                 Steve Raboin
  44|                                                               Jason Smith

   8|                         game, article that you wanted and very quickly at that! will trigger a librarian's search on your behalf. All efforts ending with you getting the information (book, DVD, cd, Wii

  23|                                                                                 FIRE DEPARTMENT
  24|                         The Firefighters and Emergency Medical Technicians of the Lunenburg Fire Department are dedicated to providing the. highest quality, most cost effective fire and emergency medical services to the citizens of the town of Lunenburg.
  25|                      As I begin to write my first annual report as your Fire Chief, I find myself reflecting on the changes that have taken place in
  26|                      the Fire Department since I first joined in 1983. In that year, the Fire Department responded to a total of 425 calls for service, with 248 being Emergency Medical Calls. At that time, the Town's population was a little over 8,000 people and the
  27|                      Fire Department consisted of a Full Time Chief and 50 on call Firefighters and EMT's. The Chief was the only person on
  28|                   • land. duty during the day. We had no apartment buildings, shopping malls or even traffic lights! Much of the town was still farm
  29|                     Fast forward to today. In 2012 your Fire Department responded to 1698 calls for service, broken down as follows:
  30|                                                                                              18

===PAGE 22=== (ocr, 42 lines)
   1|                       Fires & Hazardous Conditions                         446                   Emergency Medical          759
   2|                      Inspections                                           429                   Other                       64
   3|                      housing developments either completed, planned or under construction. Our community is now home to over 10,000 people, with several large apartment buildings, 2 shopping malls and several
   4|                      Our staffing today is 5 Career Staff and 41 On Call Firefighters and Emergency Medical Technicians. The Station is manned
   5|                      Monday through Friday from 6am-6pm. However on weeknights and weekends, all of our personnel respond from home on
   6|                      an "on call" basis. Responding from home adds an additional three to six minutes or more to our response time as
   7|                      personnel must respond to the station, pick up the appropriate equipment and then respond the call. In addition, the
   8|                      volume of calls for assistance continues to trend upward, with no particular ceiling in sight given the ongoing building in
   9|                     town. These factors lead me to continue to call for 24 hour staffing in the Station. Providing funding to staff two
  10|                      Firefighter/EMT's overnight and on weekends would allow either the ambulance or first piece of fire apparatus to respond
  11|                     immediately. Reducing the response time by four or more minutes is critical in cases of serious medical emergencies or
  12|                     structure fires. This staffing would in no way reduce the need for our staff of on call personnel; it would only supplement
  13|                     diem basis to provide the staffing. their response and reduce response times. In fact, the current recommendation is to use our existing personnel on a per
  14|                     Our firefighters continue to train for a variety of situations which we can encounter even in a town such as ours. Today's
  15|                     firefighter must be skilled in not only firefighting, but medical care, hazardous materials mitigation and technical rescue.
  16|                     They must be familiar with hazards which didn't exist even a few years ago, such as hybrid fueled vehicles, lightweight
  17|                     building construction, domestic terrorism and clandestine drug labs, just to name a few. Our EMT's must continue to stay
  18|                     abreast of the latest treatment protocols and advances in lifesaving skills. Our members train regularly both here at the
  19|                     Station and at classes sponsored by other agencies. While they are compensated for some of the training, the vast
  20|                     majority is done on their own time. To give a snapshot of some of the training required today, a new Firefighter/EMT must
  21|                    attend over 300 hours of basic firefighting training at the Massachusetts Fire Academy. In addition, they must complete
  22|                    another 140 hours of training as an Emergency Medical Technician. Once they are certified, they must complete over 50
  23|                    hours of continuing education and refresher training every two years, just for their EMT. Operators of Fire Apparatus must
  24|                    complete an additional 65 hours of training in pump and aerial operations, hydraulics calculations and vehicle operation. Again, these requirements are for call personnel, and most of this is done on their own time.
  25|                    Several of our personnel continue to maintain skills in specialized Technical Rescue, such as Cave In, Confined Space,
  26|                    Rope and Underwater Search and Recovery. These members are part of two regional teams which provide those services
  27|                    not only to Lunenburg, but the whole North County area.
  28|                    Fire Prevention activities are a major part of the Department's activity. Personnel must review and inspect all new
  29|                    construction in town for proper fire protection. In addition, there are inspections which are required by law such as Smoke
  30|                    and Carbon Monoxide Detector Installations when a home is sold and oil burner and propane gas installations. In addition
  31|                   any business holding a liquor license must be inspected each year for fire safety issues as well. Personnel also continue
  32|                   to provide fire safety education to not only children in our schools but adults as well. Several of our members also provide first aid and CPR training to both citizens and town employees.
  33|                    The transition of our Communications Center to the Nashoba Valley Dispatch Center is continuing with an anticipated
  34|                    switch over in June 2013. This center will handle all of the 911 calls and public safety communications for our town, Devens, Harvard and Lancaster.
  35|                   Several Federal Grants have been applied for and were received or are currently pending. These grants will hopefully
  36|               provided much needed training and equipment for our personnel. We continue to look for alternative sources of funding to support the services we provide.
  37|                   The rebuild of Engine 2 (our light brush truck) which was funded by Town Meeting in 2011 was completed in March. The * "
  38|                   unit's body was replaced with a lighter, more efficient body, pump and tank design. In addition, upgrades and repairs were
  39|                   made to the existing 1989 chassis. This will allow the truck to be much more efficient off road and should add several years to its lifespan.
  40|                   Town Meeting also provided funds to perform a partial overhaul of Engine 4. This unit, a 1995 pumper has been our
  41|                   workhorse since its arrival and was in need of significant work. At the time of writing, the truck's pump has been
  42|                   overhauled and repairs to the body corrosion are scheduled. These repairs, in addition to others will hopefully extend this

===PAGE 23=== (ocr, 49 lines)
   1|                            truck's lifespan another 5-6 years.
   2|                           In 2012, we saw Firefighters Alex Cloutier and TJ McCormack graduate from the Massachusetts Firefighting Academy's
   3|                           today's challenging environments. Call/Volunteer Training Program. This intensive 6 month program provides the basic skills firefighters need to operate in
   4|                          2012 also saw the retirement of two longtime members of the Department. Chief Scott Glenny retired in August 2012 after
   5|                          Firefighter in 1986, eventually rising through the ranks to Chief in July more than 37 years of service. After several years as a Call firefighter, Scott was appointed as our first "Full Time"
   6|                         2002. As Chief, Scott oversaw the construction of the current Public Safety Facility as well as significant upgrades to the
   7|                          department's apparatus fleet. Also retiring in April was Firefighter Brian LeBLanc, after more than 31 years of service. Brian not only served
   8|                          Management Director for many years. as a Firefighter and Emergency Medical Technician, but was also the Town's Emergency
   9|                         In conclusion, I would like to thank the members of the Lunenburg Fire Department for their unwavering support as l
  10|                         transitioned into the position of Chief. Their enthusiasm, professionalism and dedication truly make me proud to be their
  11|                        leader. In addition, I would like to thank the other department heads, boards and commissions for their support and
  12|                         cooperation as we work together for the good of the Town. My thanks also go out to the citizens of Lunenburg for their
  13|                         ongoing financial support. It is that support that allows us to continue providing the services you expect from us.

   5|                       accountability, of the Police Officers who are committed to providing a safe and secure environment for every citizen.

  34|                      Thank you for the opportunity to serve as your Police Chief. One of my many goals is to close any gaps in communication
  35|                      and service between the Police Department and you, the people we are sworn to protect and serve. To that end, I have been
  36|                      working to improve our Web Page and I am currently using Social media to enhance our interaction with the public and
  37|                      encourage greater transparency. I plan on setting aside a block of time during the week that will allow citizens to come by
  38|                      the police station and visit with me on a more casual basis. We're going to call this block of time "Coffee with the Chief"
  39|                      am also setting new standards within the ranks that provide the citizens of this community with the highest level of public
  40|                      service possible by improving the work environment, offering the most up to date technology and equipment, and
  41|                      developing each officer with superior training and effective leadership. Our focus will be on the needs and concerns of the
  42|                      community and are approach to law enforcement will be from the prospective of customer service as well as crime
  43|                      prevention and apprehension.
  44|                      There have been many changes within the police department this year. Congratulations to Mike Luth who was promoted to
  45|                      the rank of Lieutenant. Lt. Luth has 21 years of Law Enforcement experience, nine of which has been with the Lunenburg
  46|                      Police Department. Mike has also served with the Tyngsboro Police Department and the Secret Service. Welcome Officer
  47|                      Joshua Tocci, Officer Joshua Mathieu, and Officer Jonathan Broc to the ranks of our full time police force. The three new
  48|                     full time officers served on our Reserve force, an arm of the police department that is vital not just as a force multiplier, but
  49|                     as a vehicle to develop high quality full time police recruits. Officers Tocci, Mathieu and Broc will fill vacancies created by

===PAGE 25=== (ocr, 43 lines)
   1|                          the departure of Officer Omar Connor who left to pursue a career law enforcement job with the Town of Groton, and the
   2|                          retirements of Chief Daniel F. Bourgeois and Acting Lieutenant Stanley W. Barney. Chief Bourgeois retired in July after
   3|                          serving over 30 years of service to the town of Lunenburg. Lieutenant Barney retired as a full time officer in January of
   4|                          2013, but will stay on as a reserve officer. Stan was a member of the police department for 37 years as both a reserve and full time officer.
   5|                         Training standard requests will become a common yearly objective in Police department budget appeals not just to
   6|                         accomplish our own goals, but also to be in compliance with mandated training requirements. This will be one of the
   7|                         biggest challenges we face going forward. The Massachusetts Police Training Committee has enacted laws mandating
   8|                         important and necessary, place significant pressure on an already struggling police budget. Some of those mandates are; enhanced training standards for all police officers. The required training mandates are un-funded and costly, and although
   9|                        investigation, Breath testing, First aid and CPR, Firearms, and Domestic Violence. In-Service training for veteran officers, Basic Training for recruits, Supervisory Training, Suicide Prevention, Rape
  10|                        The transition to a regional public safety call answering point is also on the horizon. The Nashoba Valley Regional Dispatch center, which is located on Devens, is slated to go live sometime in June of 2013 with the Lunenburg Police and
  11|                        Fire representing the first and largest contributor to the new PSAP. Regionalizing Dispatch will provide a greater level of
  12|                        professionalism, enhanced communication technology, an increased amount of management oversight and costs savings
  13|                        for the community. Information regarding this change and how it will affect the way, you as the public, communicate with
  14|                       the Police and Fire departments, particularly the walk in business calls, will be much publicized as we approach the go-live
  15|                        Some grant funded projects have been met with varying degrees of success. In December the police department received
  16|                        $19,812.05 in grant funds to upgrade some of the communications infrastructure to comply with the Federal narrow
  17|                       banding mandate, build in redundancy and enhance interoperability. We have also received grant funding for traffic
  18|                       enforcement and child passenger safety. An RFP was summited to the Recreational Trails Program for the purchase of a
  19|                       four wheel multi person all-terrain vehicle to patrol the conservation lands. Other non-grant funded projects include
  20|                       creating a comprehensive school crisis policy, updating active shooter training for all sworn officers, facilitate a promotional exam, replace two patrol vehicles, and aging firearms.
  21|                       Lunenburg is still a safe place to reside or visit. However, many factors can be attributed to how the nature and type of
  22|                       crime vary; the population size and density of the community and the communities that surround us, commuting factors
  23|                       such as modes of transportation and roadway systems, education, poverty level, income level, effective strength of law
  24|                      increase in calls for service and general assistance. enforcement, and courts. A snapshot of the stats is an indication of how crime can fluctuate and how growth demands an 8000

  36|                    In 2012 calls for service were 8% higher than the previous year. General assistance calls; abandoned 911, assist citizen,
  37|                    assist the fire department, were up 14%, a 6% rise in disturbances calls, and a 67% increase in firearms licensing. Crimes
  38|                    accidents and domestic disputes against people were down by 10%, and crimes against property decreased by 3%. Arrests, larcenies, motor vehicle declined slightly. The reductions, in part, are due to better communication and
  39|                    cooperation between law enforcement agencies, investigative emphases, and intelligence led policing.
  40|                    The General assistance calls continue to rise as the Town continues to grow. Some of these calls include; Annoying phone
  41|                    calls, Are you Ok, Assist Citizen, Assist the Fire Dept., Assist other agencies, Community Policing, Business and residence security checks, Directions, Escort and transport citizens, document requests, neighbor disputes, disabled motor vehicles.
  42|                    The men and women of the police department work hard to keep pace with demands of growth, and social and
  43|                   technological change and are dedicated to making this connunity a safe place in which to reside or visit. The Police Department relies heavily on input from the public and encourages the citizens of Lunenburg report all crimes.

===PAGE 26=== (ocr, 50 lines)
   1|                                                                       DEPARTMENT OF PUBLIC WORKS
   2|                     "The mission of the Department of Public Works is to protect, preserve, and improve the Town's infrastructure and related
   3|                     assets in a manner which meets and enhances the current and future social and economic needs of the community, to
   4|                     contribute to a healthy, safe and quality environment for the Town's citizens and visitors, and to provide a cost effective
   5|                    and economically sound means of disposing of solid waste and wastewater"
   6|                     The umbrella of Public works combines the resources of the Highway, Park, Cemetery, Sewer, and Facilities divisions, and
   7|                     Vehicle Maintenance services into one department organizing and scheduling personnel and equipment to best serve the
   8|                     residents of the community through the effective delivery of an extensive scope services.
   9|                     In January, our hopes of a much need renovation of the Public Works facility were realized. The existing building at 520
  10|                     Chase Rd. was totally renovated inclusive of new office space, efficient and safe heating/cooling and ventilation. The
  11|                     installation of more efficient lighting and numerous energy saving upgrades were rewarded with a $14,000.00 rebate from
  12|                     our electrical supplier. Additionally, a separate structure was erected which allows for our entire fleet to be under cover.
  13|                     This has, as expected, decreased response time during winter events. Over time, the protection of our vehicles from severe
  14|                    weather will prove invaluable, Modernized office space allows for a professional working environment for staff and
  15|                    residents requiring service.
  16|                     It is impossible to express the level of appreciation to you the residents who supported the project and especially those
  17|                    individuals who gave so much and worked so diligently toward the success of this project. Thank you.
  18|                    PAVEMENT MANAGEMENT
  19|                     Consistent with the prioritization as determined by our Pavement Management Plan, road improvements were initiated on a
  20|                     portion of Reservoir Rd., the completion of an overlay on New West Townsend Rd to the Townsend line, a section of
  21|                    Lakefront Ave. beyond the scope of Emerald Place, and Chase Rd. (Rt. 13) from the State taking to just south of the
  22|                     Northfield Rd. intersection. The scope of the project included: a leveling course of asphalt followed by a top or finish
  23|                     course, sections of "berm" or curbing, shoulder work, minor drainage improvements and driveway aprons.
  24|                     Expanding the scope of our pavement preservation methods we added "crack sealing" to the overall program. This
  25|                     process involves the installation of rubberized asphalt under high pressure to cracks or separations in the pavement. This
  26|                     application can add approximately 5 years or more to the existing surface. The Pavement Management Plan also identifies
  27|                     specific areas where this method is the best option.
  28|                     Funding continues to be the major detriment to improving the overall condition of our roadways. The primary source of
  29|                     funding is the Massachusetts Department of Transportation Chapter 90 program. The estimated value of our 90 miles of
  30|                    paved streets is, by industry standards, is calculated at 62 million dollars. The General Highway maintenance line within the
  31|                    operational budget remains stagnant. In order to initiate and sustain a road improvement program a substantial infusion of
  32|                    funds is necessary. A conservative estimate would be approximately 1.2 million dollars per year total or an additional
  33|                    $800,000.00 to existing allocations. Applying $400,000 a year to a backlog of work that exceeds 10 million dollars places
  34|                     this department and the Town as a whole in dismal failure. Many roads are compromised to the degree that only a major
  35|                    reconstruction can bring them to an acceptable quality. Patching, or filling pot holes by DPW staff is in most areas is no
  36|                     longer the appropriate application. Without a substantial infusion of funds our roads Town-wide will continue to
  37|                    deteriorate to a point where the safety of our travelling public will be compromised and the overall quality of is life
  38|                    negatively affected.
  39|                     Line Markings
  40|                     For the first time in many years, adequate funding was made available to provide center line striping and fog lines at the
  41|                     curb on all our major roadways. Further, where applicable, crosswalks, stop bars and designated parking areas were
  42|                     repainted. By means of a supplemental allocation the Town has maintained compliance with current MUTCD mandates.
  43|                     FLEET MAINTENANCE
  44|                     The DPW is responsible for the repairs and preventative maintenance of all Town vehicles and equipment excluding the
  45|                     Fire Dept. apparatus. High performance police units, park, cemetery, and school vehicles as well as over 30 pieces of
  46|                     highway related machinery are serviced by department personnel. Outsourcing of highly technical repairs has become
  47|                     more frequent and therefore more costly. Due to the volatile global oil market, the overall cost of fuel and all petroleum
  48|                    based products continues to be a moving target. Applying a solid budget number to gasoline and diesel fuel costs is
  49|                    impossible.

  10|                        The ever increasing workload made necessary the addition of a second individual to assist with repairs and maintenance.
  11|                        The increase in staff by only one has significantly lessened the need for outsourcing. Staff remains on-call 24 hours a day
  12|                        365 days per year.
  13|                     : ENVIRONMENTAL
  14|                        Storm Water
  15|                        The Environmental Protection Agency has not yet set the requirements for Phase I! Storm water regulation and permits. As
  16|                        we await the potential of yet another unfunded Federal mandate the Town remains in compliance with current protocol.
  17|                        The DPW is forced to become reactionary to roadway drainage issues. A capital program in the 1990's earmarked
  18|                        $20,000.00 annually toward rehabilitation/installation of piping and catch basins. The elimination of this dedicated funding
  19|                        placed the financial burden upon the overall General Highway maintenance allocation effectively reducing drainage and
  20|                        roadway repairs.
  21|                        As roadways are scheduled for pavement, drainage is surveyed and necessary repairs are addressed. Little or no
  22|                        preventative maintenance, short of catch basin cleaning can be done due to a lack of available resources.
  23|                       Waste Water
  24|                        The DPW continues as the caretaker of the entire sewer system from permitting, inspections, and daily preventative
  25|                        maintenance of collector lines and pumping stations to billing and documentation. There is but one full time person who
  26|                        diligently remains on call, 24/7 365 for all sewer related emergencies.
  27|                       Landfill
  28|                        The "capped" landfill off Young's Rd. is in compliance with the Division of Environmental Protection. The site is monitored
  29|                       for excessive methane gas as well as the possibility of groundwater contamination.
  30|                       This year the frequency of the testing was reduced through a special permit acquired as a result of consistently favorable
  31|                       results. The perimeter fencing is inspected on a regular schedule by DPW personnel.
  32|                        Adjacent to the "cap" is a storage area for catch basin materials and street sweepings both determined to be hazardous
  33|                       waste. We are permitted to store these materials through the procurement of a (BUD) Beneficial Use Determination permit.
  34|                       The DEP requires yearly testing of these materials.
  35|                       On weekends during the fall and springtime months DW personnel oversee and manage the annual composting of brush,
  36|                       leaves and other organic debris providing a valuable service to our residents and add a valuable component to our
  37|                       recycling program. There has to date been no dedicated funding toward the organization or long range remediation of this
  38|                       location. Once again, dollars will be taken from the General Highway line to maintain this location while keeping it
  39|                       environmentally safe.
  40|                       Solid Waste
  41|                       Public works monitors, orders, stores, and distributes trash bags in order to facilitate the curbside collection of trash and
  42|                       recyclables by our current vendor, Casella inc.
  43|                       Lake Shirley Dam
  44|                       In the early 1990's, with the structure in need of repair/rebuilding The Lake Shirley Association turned over ownership of
  45|                       the dam to the Town. After completion of a total reconstruction, responsibilities for maintenance, monitoring, and record
  46|                       keeping was completed solely by a small number of volunteers from the Lake Association. In 2004-2005 custodianship of
  47|                       the dam, compliance with State dam regulations, the establishment of an operations manual as well as an emergency
  48|                       action plan was determined to come under the umbrella of the Department of Public Works. As with the storm water
  49|                       program and other EPA / DEP mandates compliance with cursept and future regulations is critical. We rely on the expertise

===PAGE 28=== (ocr, 48 lines)
   1|                    and support of a professional consultant to assist with predetermined mandates. The most recent inspection resulted in a
   2|                    positive report.
   3|                    WINTER OPERATIONS
   4|                     Seasonal expenditures continue to exceed allocations resulting in deficit spending. During the winter months there is no
   5|                    task more important to public safety than clearing the roadways, sidewalks and municipal lots of ice and snow in an effort
   6|                    to assure the safe passage of pedestrian and vehicular traffic, and public safety vehicles.
   7|                    Aside from the devastating storm of October which decimated our budget, the overall number of weather events was low,
   8|                    As first responders, DPW personnel with the assistance of dedicated subcontractors, work tirelessly from start to finish
   9|                    regardless of the severity or duration of the event. Nowhere is our performance more critically evaluated. Plow drivers are
  10|                    required to remain vigilant and on call, and at the ready throughout the winter season with no additional compensation.
  11|                    PARKS
  12|                    In conjunction with the three elected Park Commissioners DPW staff oversees and maintains all parks and grounds
  13|                    exclusive of school property. A full time Park Superintendent was replaced with one seasonal employee. Much of the Town
  14|                    wide landscape maintenance was outsourced resulting in a significant savings. The new multi-use track complex remains
  15|                    under the control of the Commissioners with maintenance support from DPW personnel.
  16|                    CEMETERY
  17|                   • One Cemetery Division Superintendent, a DPW employee, handles the duties of the North and South cemeteries,
  18|                    maintaining the grounds, coordinating burials, and meeting regularly with the three members of the Cemetery Commission.
  19|                    The DPW supplies equipment and support staff for, on average, forty burials a year.
  20|                    As always, I am deeply appreciative of the continued patience and support of our residents and fellow Town employees as
  21|                    we persevere through these difficult times.
  22|                    "As caretakers of the Town's infrastructure our goal is to preserve and enhance the resources of the Town of Lunenburg
  23|                    for future generations. To that end we strive to meet or exceed all expectations associated with the challenges of each of
  24|                    the divisions under the umbrella of public works"
  25|                                                                              SEWER COMMISSION
  26|                    The five member Sewer Commission was originally established at the Special Town Meeting on May 6, 2006. The Sewer
  27|                    Commission bears the responsibility of overseeing the Town's sewer system, planning for the future, and developing and
  28|                    implementing rules, regulations and policies. The Department of Public Works manages and maintains the operational side
  29|                    of the system. Under the Town's Charter, responsibility for the day-to day operation, care and maintenance of the sanitary
  30|                    sewers are under the supervision of the Town Manager. During 2012, Dave MacDonald and Butch Bilotta were re-elected
  31|                   for three year terms at the Town Election, in August our Chairman Bob Ebersole resigned, and Mike Nault was appointed in
  32|                   November to finish out his term until the May 2013 elections.
  33|                    There are currently 673 properties connected to the Lunenburg system and another 387 that have access to Town Sewer
  34|                    but are not yet connected. During 2012, we had 35 new accounts connect to Town Sewer.
  35|                    The Town of Lunenburg does not operate a sewer treatment plant. Approximately 80% of Lunenburg sewer is discharged to
  36|                   the City of Leominster, and the other 20% is discharged to the City of Fitchburg. As the rates charged by the City of
  37|                   Leominster and the City of Fitchburg remained stable during 2012, there were no rate increases to Lunenburg users since
  38|                    1/1/12. However, in order to balance the FY 13 Budget, it was necessary for the Commission to transfer $73,208 from
  39|                   retained earnings, a practice that is not sustainable. It is a goal of the Sewer Commission to present a balanced budget for
  40|                   the next fiscal year.
  41|                   The Commission continues to focus on two major issues that affect the operating costs of the sewer system; grease and
  42|                   infiltration and inflow (I/l). There has been excellent cooperation and progress made with several commercial
  43|                   establishments and the Lunenburg School system related to grease trap design and maintenance. While the Grease Trap
  44|                   Regulation, put into effect in 2011, focuses on commercial and public disposal, individual home owners can also contribute
  45|                   a significant amount of grease into the system which increases everyone's costs. We will continue to educate homeowners
  46|                   on how to protect the infrastructure and keep operating costs low by reducing their disposal of fats, oil and grease into the
  47|                system and avoiding blockages. Whether on sewer or septic, grease should not be put down the drain. The second major
  48|                                                                                            25

===PAGE 29=== (ocr, 46 lines)
   1|                            issue, ill, is usually a result of groundwater entering the system through leaks in pipes and manholes, but can also come
   2|                           from the illegal connection of roof drains or sump pumps. Ill also contributes to user costs, as the Leominster & Fitchburg
   3|                           treatment plants must accommodate the excess. The Sewer Commission is targeting these illegal sewer connections and
   4|                           faulty pipes to help reduce the l/l. To address this, a new policy has been defined that allows the Sewer Commission to
   5|                           inspect residents' homes for illegal connections, suspected as a result of inspection or camera-ing of sewer lines, and
   6|                           assess penalties to those who deny entry.
   7|                           One major goal of the Commission is to increase Sewer revenues by adding new customers, and they are very willing to
   8|                           work with the community to support sewer extensions in the areas of need as determined by the Comprehensive
   9|                           Wastewater Management Plan. One resident-driven, privately funded extension on Lancaster Ave. was proposed and
  10|                           approved. It will initially provide connections for 7 residents and is expandable to support future connections as well. There
  11|                           are other extension requests before the Commission that could provide over 50 additional users. All of these extensions as
  12|                           proposed would have very little impact to the system maintenance thereby improving the operation budget and helping to contain rates.
  13|                           The Commission is working to finalize a comprehensive review of all its governing bylaws, regulations, policies,
  14|                           procedures and fees to assure that they are consistent and meet all state and federal laws. Included in these documents is
  15|                           the updated     Sewer Extension Regulation that specifically addresses privately funded sewer extensions and the
  16|                          responsibilities & risks of the interested residents. The Lancaster Ave, project was the first application of this policy. If you
  17|                          are interested in pursuing an extension in your area, you can read about the Sewer Extension Regulation (found in the
  18|                           Policies & Procedures section of the Sewer Commission website). Another policy recently defined was establishing the
  19|                           penalty for illegal sewer connections. The minimum charge for connecting to the Sewer without the proper permits has
  20|                           been set at $5,000, and can be billed an additional $5000/day until all conditions are met to the satisfaction of the Sewer
  21|                           Commission. The Commission is planning to advertise a campaign to include an amnesty program, encouraging anyone
  22|                          with an illegal connection to come forward to avoid these potential fines. All new policies and regulations are included in
  23|                          the Sewer Commission's proposed Sewer Use Regulations which will be posted and reviewed at a Public Hearing in 2013:
  24|                          The Commission has also been working diligently to complete an updated inter-municipal agreement with the City. of
  25|                          Fitchburg. Part of this agreement will include proportional funding of Fitchburg's upgrade to the John Fitch Highway and sewer line.
  26|                          The Commission would like to thank Jack Rodriquenz, DPW Director and his staff, Corey Dowd, Sewer Technician, and Jim
  27|                          Breault, Facilities Manager, who also serves as back up, for their dedication, expertise and hard work. The Commission
  28|                          would also like to thank Barb Lefebvre for her dedication and hard work as the Business Manager for the system. Finally,
  29|                          the Commission would like to thank Bob Ebersole for his outstanding leadership during his time as member of the
  30|                          Commission and as its Chairman and wish him well in his future endeavors.
  31|                          Carl Luck, Chair                                      Mike Nault, Member
  32|                          Jay Simeone, Vice Chair                               Butch Bilotta, Member
  33|                         Dave MacDonald, Clerk
  34|                                                                               ZONING BOARD OF APPEALS
  35|                          and Special Permits for dimensional variations. The Zoning Board of Appeals heard several petitions during calendar year 2012. Applicants were seeking Special Permits
  36|                                                                                         The Zoning Board meetings are held on the second and fourth
  37|                          Wednesdays of the month on an "as needed" basis. The Zoning Board meetings are now televised on Channel 8.
  38|                          The Zoning Board consists of five member and three associate members. The members are Donald F. Bowen, Chairman,
  39|                          Raymond Beal, Vice Chairman, James Besarkarski, Alfred Gravelle and Hans Wentrup. Associate members are David Blatt,
  40|                         Paul Doherty and Sheila Lumi. Lisa Normandin serves as the Administrative Assistant to the Zoning Board.
  41|                         The functions of the Zoning Board of Appeals as required by statute are as follows:
  42|                               1. To hear and decide applications for variances from the Town of Lunenburg Protective Zoning By-laws as authorized in the by-laws.

  44|                                    decide appeals in accordance with Chapter 40A, Section 8 of the Massachusetts General Laws, as amended, from
  45|                                    any person aggrieved by reason if his or her inability to obtain a permit or enforcement action from the
  46|                                   Administrative Official. Administrative Officer or Board in the Town, the Regional Planning Agency, abutting City or Town or other

   1|                                                                   LUNENBURG PUBLIC SCHOOLS
   2|                        Our Vision: The Lunenburg Public Schools prides itself on excellence. We provide a child-centered environment that
   3|                        challenges all learners to achieve their highest potential. This is accomplished through a partnership with a supportive community.
   4|                        Our Mission: We prepare our students for life-long learning and responsible community membership.
   5|                        Our Guiding Beliefs:
   6|                                        Education is a community partnership.
   7|                                   • Learning is reflective, creative, ongoing, and takes place in all environments.
   8|                                        A successful school actively engages students and staff.
   9|                                  •     All students have talents and can excel.
  10|                                        Professional growth is fundamental to instructional excellence.
  11|                                   •    All students are capable of learning and are entitled to instructional excellence.
  12|                                        Staff and students are entitled to a safe, healthy and positive learning environment.
  13|                                  • An atmosphere of mutual respect is essential for learning.
