===PAGE 32=== (ocr, 50 lines)
   1|                                                                        SUPERINTENDENT'S MESSAGE
   2|                                                                    Loxi Jo Calmes, Superintendent of Schools
   3|                    This has been a year of changes for the Lunenburg Public Schools with the consolidation of the four schools into three
   4|                    buildings. Under the new configuration, students in grades PK-3 attend Lunenburg Primary School, grades 4-7 attend
   5|                    Turkey Hill Middle School, and grades 8-12 attend Lunenburg High School. Individual reports from each of the buildings
   6|                    chronicle the remarkable efforts of the dedicated professionals and parent volunteers who packed and unpacked over
   7|                    twenty classrooms in the spring in order to be ready for students when school began August 28, 2012. The reports also
   8|                    recognize the outstanding performance of our students, including their many events and achievements.
   9|                    The 2012 MCAS results were very positive and provided much to celebrate. Lunenburg continues to be ranked well at.
  10|                    every grade level, and this year our grade 10 students ELA performance was ranked #1 along with twenty-seven other high
  11|                    performing schools in the Commonwealth with 99% of students scoring at proficient or above. Forty-one students received
  12|                    Adams Scholarships. Student growth percentiles continue to be strong with exceptionally high growth noted in some
  13|                    areas. There is clear evidence that our focus on continuous improvement and tiered instruction are positively impacting
  14|                    student performance. We were also pleased to announce that the District was selected for the 3rd Annual Advanced
  15|                    Placement (AP) District Honor Roll. A total of 539 school districts across the U.S. and Canada were honored by the College
  16|                    Board for simultaneously increasing access to Advanced Placement® coursework while maintaining or increasing the
  17|                    Massachusetts to meet the criteria to be placed on the AP Honor Roll. Additionally, according to the College Board, more
  18|                    school districts from Massachusetts earned a spot on the honor roll than any other state in the nation. We are very proud
  19|                    of the work being done across the district to provide all students the opportunity to access rigorous curriculum.
  20|                    I was pleased to recognize an outstanding member of the class of 2013, Molly Ellen O'Connell, as this year's
  21|                    Superintendent's Award recipient. Eighth grader Ron Hyatt represented our schools and community at Governor Patrick's
  22|                    Project 351 - 2013 day of service. These two students, along with a host of their peers across the district, continue to
  23|                    demonstrate the vitality of our Mission - to prepare students for responsible community membership. Students across the
  24|                    district continue to collect for the town food bank, as well as raise awareness and funds for important causes. Of particular
  25|                    note this year are the efforts of winter track athletes who fundraised for a fellow student's education and who were also
  26|                    awarded a sportsmanship award by Massachusetts Interscholastic Athletic Association. Additionally, with the support of
  27|                    the Lunenburg PTO, Lunenburg Fire Department, Lunenburg Water Department, and numerous volunteers, the community
  28|                    ice rink was constructed. The rink was envisioned and planned by high school teachers Mr. Robert Truax and Mr. Joshua
  29|                    Koziol and students in their algebra class the previous year. Ours is indeed a caring community of learners working hard
  30|                    each day, learning and growing and giving back to the community that has supported them and their education.
  31|                    The Thomas C. Passios School continues to serve an important role hosting a variety of programs for students, as well as
  32|                    school and community services and events. The district's Extended Day, Early Learning, and School Linked Services
  33|                    programs operate in the building, as does a FLLAC collaborative day program that serves twenty-nine students from area
  34|                    schools including three from Lunenburg. In addition to the district offices, Lunenburg Cable Access has a studio at the
  35|                    school. A classroom in the school has been converted to a meeting room and professional development center in the
  36|                    building. Numerous organizations utilize the building for meetings and events including the Lunenburg PTO, Musicaiders,
  37|                    Scouts, and Junior Basketball. The Brooks House also continues to serve the district and community as well, housing the
  38|                    facilities office, a meeting room, and the local newspaper.
  39|                    This past year our schools bid farewell to four retirees who devoted many years to public service-their total tenure in
  40|                   • Lunenburg represents 60 years of service. Retiring this past year were Susan Glenny, school nurse (19 years); Al Carlson,
  41|                    maintenance (18 years); Jennifer Hood Biker, grade 8 teacher (16 years); and Christine Bonci, principal (7 years). We
  42|                    deeply appreciate the many contributions of these dedicated and committed professionals and extend our best wishes to •
  43|                    them in their retirement.
  44|                    This past year we also were deeply saddened by the passing of Elisabeth Estrada-Turner who had served the students of
  45|                    the district the past eight years as a tutor. We were also deeply impacted by the senseless loss of 20 young children and 6
  46|                    dedicated educators in Newtown, CT. The students, educators, and citizens of Lunenburg responded with an outpouring of
  47|                    love and concern in remembrance and in response to these tragic losses. We will always keep them and their families our
  48|                    thoughts.
  49|                    After five years and two regionalization studies, we are excited to be moving forward in partnership with the Massachusetts
  50|                                                                                            29

   4|                         Associates was selected as the Owner's Project Manager and Tappé is the design firm responsible for the feasibility study

   6|                         of the existing school, a renovation of and addition to the existing school, and/or new construction. The Feasibility Study
   7|                         will examine a design that provides for reconfiguring the District's 6-12 grades with a projected enrollment of 820 students

   9|                         approval of the MSBA. It is estimated that the Feasibility Study, including the Schematic Design/Final Program, will be
  10|                        completed in October 2013. The projected date for a vote by the Town is November 2013, and the execution of project

  15|                                                                         LUNENBURG PRIMARY SCHOOL
  16|                                                                          Elaine E. Blaisdell, Interim Principal
  17|                     The Lunenburg Primary School is a child-centered school devoted to providing quality early childhood education for all students.
  18|                                   We are a community of teachers, students, and parents working together to create a safe and respectful
  19|                      environment for children. Our current knowledge of child development guides the school's beliefs and practices and
  20|                      provides the foundation for student learning.
  21|                      This year the school serviced preschool to third grade students after the closing of the Thomas C. Passios Elementary
  22|                      School. We started the 2012-2013 school year with 506 students in 20 classrooms for an average class size of 23, and 41
  23|                     students in our two preschool classrooms. We constantly strive to find better ways to meet the collective as well as the
  24|                     individual needs of our students. Our Professional Development focused on Reading Comprehension, the Common Core
  25|                     curriculum, and the new teacher evaluation system. We worked with experts in the field of Literacy and established
  26|                     eighteen lab classrooms in the building that are piloting Readers Workshop and Inclusive Practices. All of our classroom
  27|                     teachers continued to use Responsive Classroom strategies as part of our Academic/Social/Emotional Learning
  28|                     Curriculum, which includes morning meetings, guided discovery, rules and logical consequences, and academic choice.
  29|                     The Primary School community continues to strive toward academic excellence. Common assessments are in place in the
  30|                     fall, winter, and spring for all children in grades K-3 in the areas of reading, writing, and math. These common
  31|                     assessments serve to monitor the effectiveness of teacher instruction and drive instruction. We are now able to track
  32|                     progress throughout the year in a faster, more efficient manner in order to improve instruction.
  33|                     The Primary School is a Title One school; we have continued our tiered instructional system to insure that all students
  34|                    have the services they need to be successful. Tiered instructional services are provided in the area of Language Arts and
  35|                     Math. The first tier is the largest, and is fitted for universal instruction in the classroom. Teachers are skilled in
  36|                     differentiating instruction to meet a large number of student needs in the classroom. The second tier is based on student
  37|                     needs that are targeted, individual, and involve more intensive interventions. The third tier is the smallest and involves
  38|                     special services with very intense interventions. The tiered instructional model requires that the teachers not only meet the
  39|                     needs of the students who may be struggling with learning but those students who are doing well and need to strive to
  40|                     achieve higher levels of learning. Learning is becoming more personalized and students are being challenged to be
  41|                    independent learners and problem solvers.
  42|                     Our staff works hard to maintain a school culture that is positive, inclusive, and supportive of each of our members. We
  43|                    have high expectations, insist on respectful behavior, and recognize the importance of building a community where
  44|                     members feel a sense of belonging and responsibility to contribute. Our students have a right to be in an environment that
  45|                    is safe, promotes learning, and enables them to achieve success.
  46|                     Student Success Night was held for all Kindergarten, Grade 1 and 2 students and their families in the spring of 2012. This
  47|                   night serves as the vehicle for students to display/articulate their strengths as a learner. To prepare for Student Success
  48|                    Night, students worked with their classroom teachers to identify and articulate their strengths in four areas: academics,
  49|                    habits of mind, creativity, and citizenship. Examples of student work in these areas were displayed in the classrooms.
  50|                    Students acted as tour guides for their families on Success Night.
  51|                    The Primary School Council meets each month to discuss school issues, review the annual budget for the school and
  52|                    develop the School Improvement Plan, as well as make revisions to the school's handbook.
  53|                    Members of the Council for the 2012-2013 school year were: were:31

   1|                        traditional special area classes students receive. This block lends itself to students having additional physical movement
   2|                        throughout the day; additional opportunities for hands-on activities; more exposure to the arts, theatre and technology; and allows them to explore their strengths and talents.
   3|                       We make a positive impact on the students we serve through offering a variety of different programs developed to promote
   4|                      . social and emotional growth, self-awareness, and community service. We continue to provide the Developmental Design
   5|                       program to grades six and seven to deliver our social emotional curriculum. This program works by students meeting with
   6|                       teachers in small groups to teach social responsibility and problem-solving skills, as well as to develop empathy and
   7|                       promote positive self-esteem. The results from this program have been a dramatic reduction in stress- and anxiety-related
   8|                      nursing referrals. Grades four and five continue to deliver Responsive Classroom strategies, which deliver a similar social.
   9|                       emotional curriculum with similar positive results. We continue to refine and enhance our programs to incorporate anti-
  10|                       bullying lessons and meet the intricate social needs of our middle school students.
  11|                       We also utilize best practices such as peer mediation and the big sibs program to foster and develop positive peer
  12|                       leadership skills and effective peer mentoring. All of the social emotional programs have been developed to allow students
  13|                       to showcase their talents and abilities, grow and improve as individuals, and promote community outreach for the younger
  14|                       Turkey Hill Middle School for the third straight year is a Great Books Foundation Model School, based on our excellence in
  15|                      instruction and ability to provide meaningful opportunities for the development of critical thinking skills. For the past three
  16|                      years our faculty has been collaborating with the Great Books program experts on how to refine our instruction and
  17|                      improve writing skills throughout the curriculum. The feedback provided by the model school experts is positive and THMS
  18|                      continues to excel in the targeted areas of writing and critical thinking when compared to other model schools across the
  19|                      country. This year, Turkey Hill Middle School will host a "Learning Walk" in which area educators from throughout the state
  20|                      will come to THMS to observe and learn how to implement the successful strategies that our teachers are currently using.
  21|                      Our teaching staff will also be videotaped in order to be used as a training tool for other aspiring educators.
  22|                      Turkey Hill Middle School continues to provide educational services and support that ensure student success and promote
  23|                      lifelong learning and responsible community membership. The key components to our success are our talented faculty
  24|                      who are committed to making a positive impact on students, our parents and community who support our teaching and
  25|                      learning, and most of all, our students who inspire us through their commitment to learning. Together we continue to make a difference in the lives of students.

  20|                                                                           LUNENBURG HIGH SCHOOL
  21|                                                                              Brian Spadafino, Principal
  22|                    Lunenburg High School was a hub for academic and social activity in 2012-2013. Even before school started, there was a
  23|                    lot of change swirling around LHS. We have a new administrative team in place and, as a result of the district-wide
  24|                    consolidation, we welcomed nine members to the LHS staff. We also hired two teachers to the LHS faculty and the district.
  25|                    A transition team of five teachers met this summer to plan ways to ensure that student and staff needs would be met. With
  26|                    the assistance of 44 student leaders, we successfully transitioned almost 250 eighth and ninth grade students into the LHS
  27|                    culture by having an eighth and ninth grade orientation on the first day of school. We opened the year, as we do every
  28|                    year, with a guest speaker from the Worcester County District Attorney's Office to discuss responsible Internet use,
  29|                    Facebook, cyber-bullying and Internet safety with our incoming students and parents.
  30|                    NEASC ACCREDITATION
  31|                    Lunenburg High School recently completed the Two Year Progress Report for the Commission on Public Secondary
  32|                   Schools of the New England Association of Schools and Colleges. In 2010, the Commission recommended that the school
  33|                   receive continued accreditation in the Association. The Commission's decision was based upon review of an evaluation
  34|                   report prepared by a visiting committee in April 2010.
  35|                   While the Commission lauded LHS in many areas, the Commission warned HS in three areas. Of major concern were
  36|                  facility and technology issues. The Commission stated that the physical plant significantly limits the school's ability to
  37|                   meet the educational needs of students, and that there is an expectation to develop both a short- and long-term plan to
  38|                   fund and address the significant facilities concerns including infrastructure and wiring issues, roof and window issues,
  39|                   limited handicap accessibility, and inadequate space. In curriculum, the commission recommended the further.
  40|                   development of a standard template to clearly articulate school-wide expectations for student learning.
  41|                   As part of the Two Year Progress Report, a committee was assembled to address the recommendations that were made by
  42|                   the NEASC visiting committee. Three teachers and two administrators went through the NEASC report and needed to
  43|                   address whether the recommendations were: completed, in progress, or planned for the future. The Commission identified
  44|                   nine Highlighted recommendations. Of the Highlighted recommendations, two were deemed "complete" and seven were
  45|                   deemed "in progress." One of the major recommendations that needed to be addressed was the issue of the LHS facility.
  46|                   Twenty-seven other recommendations were also addressed in the Two Year Progress Report. The report was submitted to :
  47|                   the Commission on January 1, 2013.
  48|                                                                                         35

   1|                          SCHOOL/COMMUNITY PARTNERSHIPS
   2|                          In March, the National Honor Society will be having its annual Blood Drive. Students sixteen and over will be able to
   3|                          participate in the Blood Drive with parent permission.
   4|                          In April, Students Against Destructive Decisions (SADD) will host former Boston Celtic player, Chris Herren. Chris has
   5|                          struggled with substance abuse for much of his basketball career. He has been the subject of books (Fall River Dreams
   6|                          and Basketball Junkie), as well as the subject of the Emmy Award nominated ESPN documentary Unguarded. Sober. since
   7|                          2008, Chris will share his story of abuse and recovery and share pitfalls that our students might face. All students in
   8|                          grades eight through twelve will attend the assembly.

  11|                          instructional and assessment practices, curriculum work, and hard work by students and teachers.
  12|                                                                       Lunenburg High School - 2012 MCAS Results
  13|                                         Subject Test                Advanced/Proficient                  State Rank               Percentile in State

  17|                         Using combined percentage bands of the advanced and proficient range, LHS scored 100% in ELA. In Mathematics, LHS
  18|                         scored 91% advanced/proficient. In Science/Technology/Engineering, LHS scored 82% advanced/proficient. We are
  19|                         extremely proud of, our students and teachers for their continued improvement, and we will work to move all students to
  20|                         the level of proficiency as measured by MCAS and our goal of college and career ready as they move through our curriculum.
  21|                       • We were also very pleased to learn that 41 seniors at Lunenburg High School received the state's John and Abigail Adams
  22|                         Scholarship this year. The scholarship is available to students whose MCAS performance puts them at the top 25 percent
  23|                         of their district. To be eligible, students must either score Advanced on one exam and Proficient on the other, or Advanced
  24|                        on both the English and Math exams. We also had two students who were recognized as commended students in the
  25|                        National Merit Scholarship competition.

  32|                        Class Plays delighted the audience, and the musical, Wizard of Oz, and the Pops are scheduled for the spring. One student

   3|                    CURRICULUM
   4|                   The staff at Lunenburg High School focused their professional development activities in 2012-2013 working on curriculum
   5|                   development, curriculum alignment to the Common Core State Standards, and implementation of the new teacher
   6|                    evaluation system. The role of the department head has changed this year; a leadership team has been formed, which
   7|                   includes the department heads and the administration. Five meetings a month are held. The focus of the team is to
   8|                   improve our curriculum, instructional practices, and assessment. The main goals for the team members are to gain an
   9|                   understanding of the Common Core State Standards, be able to articulate their understanding to department members, and
  10|                   to ensure that our curriculum maps are updated to reflect the changes.

  10|                                                                                     SCHOOL FACILITIES
  11|                                                                                John Londa, Facilities Director
  12|                         Planning for renovations or replacement of Lunenburg High School, or the development of a middle school / high school
  13|                         building, continued to move forward with the approval at the May 2012 Annual Town Meeting of $750,000 for the feasibility
  14|                         study. Between July and September, the School Building Committee approved a request for qualifications for an owner's
  15|                         project manager and interviewed qualified candidates. In October 2012, Joslin, Lesser + Associates was selected as the
  16|                         owner's project manager for the project at a cost of $125,000. Between December 2012 and February 2013, a request for
  17|                         qualifications for a designer was released, architectural firms submitted their qualifications, and the selection board
  18|                        selected three finalists to interview. On February 5, 2013, the Massachusetts School Building Authority designer selection panel designated Tappé Associates as the top-ranked finalist.
  19|                         Due to significant restraints on the School Department operational budget in FY13, the School Committee at the end of the
  20|                         prior school year approved the consolidation of the district to three school buildings. Lunenburg Primary School was
  21|                        configured to grades Pre-K to 3, the Turkey Hill Middle School to grades 4-7, and Lunenburg High School to grades 8-12.
  22|                        The Thomas C. Passios Elementary School became home to the Caldwell School, a special services school of the FLLAC
  23|                        Collaborative, which rented roughly 1/3 of the space in the school. The remainder of the space was used by the district's
  24|                        extended daycare program, the district administration, and Lunenburg Public Access Cable. The consolidation of the
  25|                        classroom furniture. schools was completed in July and August, with the custodial and maintenance staff relocating teacher materials and some
  26|                        The FY13 capital plan approved the re-seaming of the existing 25-year-old rubber membrane roof on the Thomas C. Passios
  27|                        Elementary School in order to extend the life of the roof. Haynes, Leineck, and Smith, Inc. was the project designer at a
  28|                        cost of $9,098. The project was completed by M.D.M. Engineering in November 2012 at a cost of $73,000. Snow guards
  29|                       were added to the Turkey Hill Middle School gym to safeguard against snow loads falling from the gym roof and causing
  30|                        injury to someone, and in order to protect lower elevation roofs. The Passios roof work and the Middle School snow guard
  31|                       work were bid together with M.D.M. Engineering completing the snow guard work at a cost of $18,010.
  32|                        The focus of the school maintenance effort in light of the FY13 budget is to ensure that all school buildings continue to
  33|                        support education by ensuring as rapid a response as possible is made to complete work orders for repairs, to continue to
  34|                        ensure a preventative maintenance program, and when possible, security and minor improvements to energy efficiency.
  35|                       Work to improve the appearance to spaces such as carpet replacement, painting, replacement of furniture, upgrades to
  36|                       the first seven months of the year, 437 work orders were completed. fixtures and other new work is currently not possible without sacrificing preventative maintenance or support for repairs: In
  37|                       For the FY14 Capital Program, the School Department requested $13,800 for each of three school buildings to upgrade the
  38|                       front end controller to the HVAC control systems as its highest priority, $15,000 for asbestos remediation at three schools,
  39|                       and $16,000 to replace flooring in the Lunenburg Primary School cafeteria and teacher lunch room. Armstrong Flooring,
  40|                       Replacement of the floor is needed due to water vapor causing the linoleum to bubble. Inc. has agreed to assist by providing materials for the replacement of the linoleum with vinyl composition tile.
  41|                                                                                               38

===PAGE 42=== (ocr, 47 lines)
   1|                        MONTACHUSETT REGIONAL VOCATIONAL TECHNICAL SCHOOL
   2|                                                                     Steven C. Sharek, Superintendent-Director
   3|                                                                                         Overview
   4|                      The year 2011-2012 was an exciting one at Monty Tech, marked by student achievement, faculty and staff distinctions, and
   5|                     leadership development. I am honored to serve as Superintendent-Director of Monty Tech, a school that continues to
   6|                     transform secondary education in North Central Massachusetts, bringing to life lessons learned in a classroom, at the
   7|                     workplace, and in the community. This school exemplifies what I believe good education is all about.
   8|                     A Monty Tech education is grounded in workforce preparedness, balanced by a rigorous academic curriculum. Our
   9|                     students are prepared not only to enter the workforce, but to also continue their education by enrolling in college. Each
  10|                     year Monty Tech conducts a One-Year-Out Survey, measuring "positive placements," as defined by the Carl D. Perkins
  11|                     Vocational and Technical Education Act. By tracking students who have enrolled in post-secondary education programs,
  12|                     secured employment aligned to their vocational training, or are serving our country in the United States military, we are
  13|                     better able to inform potential students, advise current students, and deliver vocational-technical programs that are
  14|                    relevant to today's workforce trends and labor market needs. One year after leaving Monty Tech, the graduates of 2011
  15|                    reported the following:
  16|                               33% are currently employed in a field related to their Monty Tech trade
  17|                               12% are employed in a field unrelated to their vocational education
  18|                                45% are currently pursuing a higher education, and
  19|                               8% have elected to serve in the United States military
  20|                    Compiling information for the Annual Report has provided me with an opportunity to reflect on the achievements and
  21|                    activities of our students, faculty and staff. I am fortunate to work closely with a talented leadership team, many of whom
  22|                    have contributed to this report, and given great insight into some of the more notable highlights of the 2011-2012 academic
  23|                    year, including:
  24|                    • 95% of our students scored Advanced or Proficient in the state's English Language Arts exam, up from 87% in 2010-
  25|                          2011.
  26|                       88% of our students scored Advanced or Proficient in the state's mathematics exam, up from 87% in 2010-2011.
  27|                    • Class of 2012 graduate Victoria Holbert served as the National SkillsUSA President, traveling across the country and
  28|                          abroad to advocate for vocational-technical education. She participated in the "Education Nation Summit," engaging in high-level discussions on the state of the American Education system.
  29|                    • Seven students advanced through local, district and state competitions to earn the right to compete at the National
  30|                          SkillsUSA Conference in Kansas City, Missouri. The Monty Tech national delegation was awarded four gold medals
  31|                          and two bronze medals.
  32|                    • For the third consecutive year, the Monty Tech Marine Corps JROT program was awarded the distinction of "Naval
  33|                         Honor School," in recognition of exemplary community service.
  34|                    • One outstanding JROTC Cadet was awarded the Naval Reserve Officers Training Corps Scholarship, receiving full
  35|                          tuition, fees, books, uniforms, summer experiences, as well as monthly stipends. She is now attending Norwich
  36|                          University.
  37|                    • Monty Tech continues to participate in the highly selective Student Spaceflight Experiment Program, representing the
  38|                          only vocational school in the nation to have a student science experiment launch into space, and providing students
  39|                          with an opportunity to study the effects of gravity on their science project.
  40|                    Much like districts across the state, Monty Tech has been affected by the nationwide economic recession. Maintaining
  41|                    high standards of fiscal responsibility, with an eye on our over-arching mission of increasing student achievement, this
  42|                    school has been able to maintain our high standards of academic success, while presenting a budget that is fiscally
  43|                    conservative. Our FY12 Annual Budget was unanimously accepted by each of the eighteen member communities, and
  44|                    reflected a 2.4% increase over the 2010-2011 Educational Plan. We deeply appreciate the public's support for Monty Tech.
  45|                    We recognize that we are in the midst of some of the most difficult economic times faced by residents of our member cities
  46|                    and towns. As a result, we will continue to strive to seek creative ways to keep down costs to our 18 member communities.
  47|                                                                                           39

   1|                                                                                     Our Mission
   2|                   Every student will graduate from Montachusett Regional Vocational Technical School with the skills, knowledge, and
   3|                   abilities to be a productive and effective member of an ever-changing society.
   4|                                                                                     Our District
   5|                   Montachusett Regional Vocational Technical School is a four-year career and technical high school serving the member
   6|                   towns of:
   7|                      Ashburnham                                        Harvard                                         • Princeton
   8|                     Ashby                                              Holden                                            Royalston
   9|                     Athol                                              Hubbardston                                       Sterling
  10|                      Barre                                             Lunenburg                                         Templeton
  11|                      Fitchburg                                         Petersham                                          Westminster
  12|                      Gardner                                           Phillipston                                        Winchendon
  13|                                                                                     Leadership
  14|                      The leadership team at Montachusett Regional Vocational Technical School is comprised of ten talented administrators
  15|                      whose varied educational backgrounds, professional experiences, and areas of expertise contribute to the success of
  16|                      the school. Working collaboratively, and under the direction of the Superintendent and Principal, the team has been
  17|                      able to transform the school into one of the most sought-after high schools in North Central Massachusetts.
  18|                      Nicholas DeSimone, Principal                                                Richard Nutt, Director of Vocational Programs
  19|                      Francine Duncan, Director of Technology                                     Steven C. Sharek, Superintendent-Director
  20|                      Christina Favreau, Director of Academic Programs                            Katy Whitaker, Development Coordinator
  21|                      James Hachey, Dean of Admissions                                            Victoria Zarozinski, Director of Student Support
  22|                      Richard Ikonen, Director of Facilities                                      Services
  23|                      Tammy Lajoie, Business Manager
  24|                                                                                      Enrollment
  25|                      On October 1, 2011, student enrollment at Monty Tech included 1,435 students in grades nine through twelve. Students
  26|                      are represented from every community in the district: Ashburnham (58), Ashby (37), Athol (110), Barre (39), Fitchburg
  27|                      (376), Gardner (173), Harvard (4), Holden (60), Hubbardston (56), Lunenburg (72), Petersham (8), Phillipston (17),
  28|                      Princeton (14), Royalston (24), Sterling (58), Templeton (110), Westminster (70), and Winchendon (127).
  29|                      Throughout 2011-2012, Monty Tech offered a variety of opportunities for students, parents, and community members to
  30|                      learn about and visit the school. In October 2011, approximately 1,000 district eighth graders participated in the annual
  31|                      "Tour Day" event. Students toured our twenty vocational/technical areas and learned about the school's challenging
  32|                      academic offerings and exciting athletic and extracurricular programs. Career Awareness Night offered interested
  33|                      students the opportunity to return in the evening with their family members, to further explore the facilities and talk with
  34|                      staff members.
  35|                      Each year, the Dean of Admissions conducts school visits, student interviews, and accepts applications for
  36|                      admissions. 2011-2012 proved to be an exceptionally busy year for him, as the school received a record 823
  37|                      applications for admission. Of those, 773 were from students hoping to enter our incoming freshman class. The
  38|                       balance of the applications came from students hoping to enter the school as upperclassmen. Because there are only
  39|                       a limited number of students the school can accept each year, 380 freshmen and 24 upperclassmen were admitted.
  40|                       The Vocational Interest Program (V.I.P.) offers area seventh and eighth grade students the chance to visit Monty Tech
  41|                       after school, and participate in hands-on learning experiences across a variety of vocational/technical areas. The
  42|                       program continued to attract a large number of students during the 2011-2012 school year, serving approximately 675
  43|                       area students.
  44|                                                                                            40

===PAGE 44=== (ocr, 38 lines)
   1|                                                                           Class of 2012 Awards
   2|                 Members of the Class of 2012 were awarded approximately $50,000 in scholarships. The Monty Tech Foundation
   3|                 generously provided $21,000 in scholarships to graduating seniors, ranging in amounts of $100 to $1,500.
   4|                 Foundation also awarded $6,000 to the practical nursing graduates. Once again, local and state organizations, as well
   5|                 as generous individuals, continue to recognize the ability and potential of Monty Tech graduates in the form of financial
   6|                 donations. The School Committee, administration, faculty, and graduates themselves, are grateful for this support.
   7|                 Articulation Agreements with local colleges also play an important role in helping reduce the cost of higher education.
   8|                 Qualified Monty Tech students are eligible to receive college credits through a number of articulation agreements with
   9|                 public and private colleges across the country. Just over 70% of the graduating class of 2012 reported plans to enroll
  10|                 at either a 2-year college, 4-year college/university, or a technical/trade school upon graduation. By earning college
  11|                 credits while still in high school, these students will benefit by saving both time and money as they pursue advanced
  12|                 educational programs.
  13|                                                                              Financial Report
  14|                 Numerous challenges were faced during the development of the fiscal year 2011-2012 budget, including rising health
  15|                 insurance and student transportation costs. Considerable effort was put forth by the School Committee,
  16|                 administration, and staff to develop a cost-effective budget. The final fiscal year 2011-2012 Educational Plan totaled
  17|                 $22,744,779, which represents a $2.4% increase over the 2010-2011 Educational Plan.
  18|                 The District was audited in August 2012 as part of the yearly financial audit by the accounting firm of Melanson, Heath
  19|                 and Co. from Greenfield, MA and an "excellent. report is anticipated.
  20|                                                                           Grants and Contracts
  21|                 Monty Tech continues to pursue grant funding on an annual basis. These funds assist in providing many educational
  22|                 and social services to the student population. For fiscal year 2012, state and federal grant sources provided the school
  23|                 with $1,407,959. Programs funded by these grants include: Essential Health Services, Social Intervention and
  24|                 Mediation, Improving Teacher Quality, Special Education Services and Program Improvement, Title | Support, Drug Free
  25|                 Schools, Perkins Occupational Education, and Summer Academic Support.
  26|                 In addition to state and federal grant sources, Monty Tech was also the recipient of private and competitive grant
  27|                 awards totaling $20,918. For Fiscal Year 2012, Monty Tech received $13,251 in grant funds from the Community
  28|                 Foundation of North Central Massachusetts, to support the Student Spaceflight Experiment Program. The
  29|                 Massachusetts Space Grant Consortium and Nypro also contributed to this ground-breaking program, by adding an
  30|                 additional $7,667 in grant funds.
  31|                 Grants totaled $1,428,877 for fiscal year 2012.

  26|                                                                              Special Services
  27|                 During the 2011-2012 school year, Montachusett Regional Vocational Technical School District provided special
  28|                 services to over three hundred students - measuring progress of approximately two hundred students on Individual
  29|                 Education Plans (IEPs) and just over one hundred students adhering to individualized 504 plans. While the Student
  30|                 Support Services (SSS) Department encompasses special education, the department provides support and is available
  31|                 to all Monty Tech students.
  32|                 The department incudes a full-time nursing staff that administers medications, performs state-mandated health
  33|                 screening exams, and provides, when necessary, health information to the special education team for a student's IEP
  34|                meeting. The department is fortunate to be able to support a full-time school social worker who participates in
  35|                 departmental meetings and assists students who have needs concerning finances, family issues, homelessness,
  36|                 maternity, health issues, and proper food and clothing. The school is fortunate to have on staff a full-time psychologist,
  37|                whose role it is to evaluate all students referred for an initial evaluation or who require a three-year re-evaluation. In
  38|                 addition, we have a full-time speech pathologist, who is available to assist students with disabilities, assess these
  39|                 students and consult with teachers. Our students also have access to the services of a full-time adjustment counselor
  40|                and part-time school psychologist. All of these individuals are available for scheduled counseling sessions and mental
  41|                health emergency treatment, as well as crisis intervention.
  42|                The school's Director of Student Support Services oversees the district's Special Education Program, which is
  43|                reviewed annually in May, in accordance with regulatory requirements. This comprehensive review and evaluation is
  44|                done in collaboration with the Parent Advisory Council, and the results of the evaluation are used to improve the
  45|                special education procedures and programs in place at Montachusett Regional Vocational Technical School.
  46|                                                                                      47

  26|                            • Five Cadets attended advance leadership training in Boswell, Pennsylvania
  27|                            • Unit conducted in excess of six thousand hours of community service
  28|                                                                                Women in Technology
  29|                      This year, the Women In Technology Program (WIT) observed its 10th anniversary of providing opportunities for young
  30|                      women from high schools located throughout the district to learn firsthand about careers in high-tech and business.
  31|                       Qualified applicants spent two days a month working on real-world work projects under the mentorship of company
  32|                      managers at SimplexGrinnell and Tyco Safety Products, subsidiaries of Tyco International, a Fortune 500 company.
  33|                      Students from six area high schools - Monty Tech, Leominster's Center for Technical Education, Oakmont, Quabbin,
  34|                    • Gardner and Narragansett - participate in the program, working on a wide variety of projects. Their projects are
  35|                      unveiled at an annual end-of-the-year presentation held in Monty Tech's Performing Arts Center. Corporate sponsors,
  36|                      along with families, friends and teachers, are invited to attend and see what the students have accomplished.
  37|                      This long-standing program continues to offer opportunities to young ladies interested in gaining work experience in a
  38|                      corporate setting. Affiliation with the program and skills acquired through participation in the Women in Technology
  39|                      program will open doors to career opportunities not otherwise available to high school students. Graduates of the
  40|                      program are also equipped with a foundation to better meet the challenges of an ever-changing and demanding work
  41|                     force.
  42|                                                                                            49

===PAGE 54=== (ocr, 36 lines)
   1|                                                                         Postgraduate and Continuing Studies
   2|                        The Postgraduate & Continuing Studies Program, also known as Monty Tech Nites, continues to update and add
   3|                        educational experiences. courses that emphasize a commitment to excellence through the provision of affordable, quality, and enjoyable
   4|                        For the Fall of 2011, there were 108 postgraduate and continuing education courses offered, with approximately 954
   5|                        seats sold. There were 106 courses offered during the Spring 2012 semester, with over 960 seats sold.
   6|                       In March 2012 the postgraduate program successfully graduated                       our first   class of fifteen Emergency Medical
   7|                       Technicians. The students took their practical exam at Monty Tech and proceeded to take their written exam at a state-
   8|                       designated facility. Several graduates have already found employment in their chosen field.
   9|                       The Director of the Postgraduate & Continuing Studies Program actively seeks information to develop new (and expand
  10|                       existing) certificate and licensure programs that align with regional workforce needs and employment trends.
  11|                                                                             Practical Nursing Program
  12|                       The Practical Nursing Program is a post-secondary licensure program, offered through the Postgraduate & Continuing
  13|                       Studies Department. The widely popular program is designed to prepare graduates to practice safely, ethically and in a
  14|                      caring manner for patients who are experiencing common variations in health status in diverse health care settings.
  15|                      In October 2010, Monty Tech's Practical Nursing Program was reviewed by the Massachusetts Board of Nursing and
  16|                      received full approval status. The program has since maintained that accreditation, and in June 2011 the program
  17|                     is scheduled for a full review and site visit by COE in November 2012. became a candidate for accreditation by the Council on Occupational Education (COE). The Practical Nursing Program
  18|                     In June 2012, the Practical Nursing Program graduated twenty-eight students bound for the nursing profession. Upon
  19|                      completion of the program, the cohort achieved an initial NCLEX pass rate of 94%, up from 87.5% in 2011. The program
  20|                     is pleased to announce that over one third of our graduates have elected to continue their education, in pursuit of a
  21|                     bachelor's degree in nursing through the LN to BSN Bridge program at Fitchburg State University.
  22|                                                                       The Monty Tech School Committee

  24|                    individuals, whose expertise proves invaluable in advising the district's operations, policies, and procedures.

   1|                       TOWN OF LUNENBURG
   2|                       ANNUAL TOWN MEETING WARRANT
   3|                       MAY 5, 2012
   4|                       The Annual Town Meeting was called to order at 9:15 AM in the Lunenburg High School Auditorium. The Moderator
   5|                       noted the presence of a quorum and that the notice of the meeting was duly posted. The Pledge of Allegiance was
   6|                       recited and then the High School Acapella group sang God Bless America. The moderator went over the ground rules
   7|                       and thanked the retirees since our last meeting; Albert Carlson, School, 19 years, Judith Schenck, 14 years, Scott
   8|                       Curtis, School, 14 years, Ernest Gould, Police, 24 years and Peter Fiandaca Highway, 11 years. At 9:25 AM action
   9|                       began on Article 1 of the Special Town Meeting.
  10|                       ARTICLE 1. To see if the Town will vote to raise and appropriate, or transfer from available funds $6,595.98 for payment
  11|                       of prior year expenses; or take any action relative thereto. [Submitted by the Town Accountant] Board of Selectmen and
  12|                       Finance Committee recommendation approval. VOTED UNANIMOUSLY to transfer the sum of $5,606.98 from Line 13,
  13|                       Reserve Fund, of Article 20 of the May 7, 2011 Annual Town Meeting to fund Prior Year Expenses.
  14|                       ARTICLE 2. To see if the Town will vote to appropriate tol from the Stabilization Fund; or take any action relative
  15|                       thereto. [Submitted by the Town Manager] Board of Selectmen and Finance Committee to make recommendations at
  16|                       Town Meeting. ARTICLE 2 was PASSED OVER.
  17|                       ARTICLE 3. To see if the Town will vote to appropriate from available funds all sums of money necessary to amend the
  18|                       amounts voted for the Town's FY12 Budget, under Article 20 of the May 7, 2011 Annual Town Meeting and Article 1 of
  19|                       the December 5, 2011 Special Town Meeting; or take any action relative thereto. [Submitted by the Town Manager]
  20|                       Board of Selectmen and Finance Committee to make recommendations at Town Meeting. ARTICLE 3 was PASSED
  21|                       ARTICLE 4. To see if the Town will vote to rescind the sum of $833,370.13, the remaining borrowing authorization, from
  22|                       Article 16 of the May 6, 2006 Annual Town Meeting for engineering, permitting, and construction of drinking water and

  20|                    ARTICLE 17. To see if the Town will vote to amend the Zoning Bylaw by adding to the current bylaw Section 4.0, entitled
  21|                    "Use Regulations," a new Section entitled Section 4.15, "Solar Energy Systems," or take any other action relative
  22|                    thereto, as follows. [Submitted by the Planning Board] Board of Selectmen recommend approval. Finance Committee
  23|                    reports no direct financial impact.
  24|                    Section 4.15 SOLAR ENERGY SYSTEMS
  25|                    The purpose of this section is to provide siting for solar photovoltaic energy systems for power generation for all,
  26|                    home, commercial or industrial installations. Small-Scale Ground-Mounted Photovoltaic Installations, Roof-Mounted
  27|                    Solar Energy Installations and Large- Scale Ground-Mounted Photovoltaic Installations within the Solar Overlay District
  28|                    may be constructed without need for discretionary approval as set forth herein. Large-Scale Ground-Mounted
  29|                    Photovoltaic Installations outside of the Solar Overlay District may be constructed by special permit provided certain
  30|                    criteria as set forth herein are met. The provisions in this section of the Zoning Bylaw shall apply to the construction,
  31|                    operation, repair, and/or removal of all solar electric systems, and to physical modifications that materially alter the
  32|                    type, configuration, or size of these installations or related equipment.
  33|                    4.15.2 Definitions
  34|                    Small-Scale Ground-Mounted Photovoltaic Installation: A solar photovoltaic system that is structurally mounted on the
  35|                    ground and has a nameplate capacity under 10 kW. Large-Scale Ground-Mounted Photovoltaic Installation: A solar
  36|                    photovoltaic system that is structurally mounted on the ground and has a nameplate capacity of 10 kW or greater.
  37|                    Rated Nameplate Capacity: The maximum rated output of electric power production of the photovoltaic system in Direct
  38|                    Current (DC). Solar Photovoltaic Array: An arrangement of solar photovoltaic panels. Roof-Mounted Solar Energy
  39|                    Installation: Solar photovoltaic arrays placed on the roof of residences, or commercial, industrial or institutional
  40|                    buildings and Town owned municipal buildings. Buffer Strip: A strip of land between the solar photovoltaic arrays, and
  41|                    any structures accessory thereto, and the boundary of the parcel, reserved for plant material, berms, walls or fencing to
  42|                    serve as a visual barrier. Plant Material: Trees or shrubs of a type and height that sufficiently screen the arrays without
  43|                    blocking necessary sun that would hinder the product performance, including ground cover that would screen the
  44|                    lower portion of the arrays.
  45|                    4.15.3 Small-Scale Ground-Mounted Solar Photovoltaic Installations and Roof-Mounted Solar Energy Installations
  46|                    4.15.3.1 Purpose
  47|                    The purpose of section is to promote the creation of renewable energy for individual residences, commercial
  48|                    enterprises and municipal buildings, as-of-right.
  49|                    4.15.3.2 Roof-Mounted Solar Energy Installations
  50|                    Roof-Mounted Solar Energy Installations shall be located so as not to increase the total height of the structure more
  51|                    than one (1) foot above the applicable zoning regulations related to height in the District in which it is located, or such
  52|                                                                                       81

===PAGE 85=== (ocr, 57 lines)
   1|                          other height as is determined by the Building/Zoning Official to be essential for proper operation, but in no case no
   2|                          more than four (4) feet.
   3|                          4.15.3.3 Small-Scale Ground-Mounted Solar Energy Installations
   4|                          Small-Scale Ground-Mounted Photovoltaic Installation are not permitted in the Zoning Dimensional Setbacks as listed
   5|                         in Section 5.0, Subsection 5.2 of the Zoning Bylaw and may not be located closer than 25 feet from residential side-yard
   6|                         lines. Dimensional setbacks for Small-Scale Ground-Mounted Photovoltaic Installation shall have a front lot setback no
   7|                          closer than the existing foundation of the primary dwelling to the front property line, except when the existing
   8|                         foundation of the primary dwelling is more than 125 feet from the front property line or otherwise if determined
   9|                         appropriate by the Building/Zoning Official. Small-Scale Ground-Mounted Photovoltaic Installation shall be adequately
  10|                         screened from the neighboring lot line as determined by the Building/Zoning Official. A Small-Scale Ground-Mounted
  11|                         Photovoltaic Installation with twenty feet or greater in height, at its maximum extension, shall require a Special Permit
  12|                         in accordance with Section 4.15.4.3.
  13|                         4.15.3.4 Required Documents
  14|                         The following documents shall be required:
  15|                         A. Sun and shadow diagrams specific to the proposed installation to determine the solar access.
  16|                         B. Detailed information, including maps, plans or dimensional sketches showing proposed location of the solar
  17|                         installation, including any setbacks from property lines or distances from structures which are used for habitation on
  18|                         adjacent properties, and a landscape diagram showing proposed screening.
  19|                         C. Site drawings showing the building and structure footprints, property lines, location and the dimensions of solar
  20|                         arrays, ridgeline of roof and description of the installation.
  21|                         D. Elevation drawings showing heights of buildings and solar arrays.
  22|                        4.15.3.5 Permitting
  23|                         Small-Scale Ground-Mounted Solar Photovoltaic Installations and Roof-Mounted Solar Energy Installations require only
  24|                         a building permit, except that a Small-Scale Ground-Mounted Solar Photovoltaic Installation twenty feet in height or
  25|                         greater, at its maximum extension, shall require a special permit in accordance with Section 4.15.4.3. All data listed
  26|                         above shall be subritted to the Building/Zoning Official. All other necessary permits shall be obtained before a building
  27|                         permit is issued.
  28|                         4.15.3.6 As Built Plans
  29|                        As-built plans shall be submitted prior to final inspection to the Building/Zoning Official and copied to the Planning
  30|                         Board.
  31|                         4.15.4 Large-Scale Ground-Mounted Solar Photovoltaic Installations
  32|                         4.15.4.1 Purpose
  33|                         The purpose of this section is to facilitate the creation of new Large-Scale Ground-Mounted Solar Photovoltaic
  34|                        Installations by providing standards for the placement, design, construction, operation, monitoring, modification and
  35|                        removal of such installations that address public safety, minimize impacts on environmental, scenic, natural and
  36|                         historic resources and to provide adequate financial assurance for the eventual decommissioning of such installations.
  37|                        Subject to the requirements below, Large-Scale Ground-Mounted Solar Photovoltaic Installations are permitted as-of-
  38|                        right in the Solar Overlay District and by special permit in any other district. The special permit granting authority for
  39|                        these purposes shall be the Planning Board.
  40|                        4.15.4.2 Solar Overlay District
  41|                        Large-Scale Ground-Mounted Solar Photovoltaic Installations are allowed by right in the following designated Overlay
  42|                        District:
  43|                        1.27 Youngs Road, 42 Acres, Map 115, Parcel 016 (Commercial District)
  44|                        2. 671 Lancaster Avenue, 132 Acres, Map 110, Parcel 3 (Residence B)
  45|                        3. 500 Leominster Shirley Road, 17.4 Acres, Map 144, Parcel 001 (Office Park and Industrial).
  46|                        4.15.4.3 Siting By Special Permit
  47|                        Except where permitted as-of-right in the Solar Overlay District, Large-Scale Ground-Mounted Solar Photovoltaic
  48|                        Installations may be located on any lot containing more than 20 acres in any district by special permit issued by the
  49|                        Planning Board, provided however that, the requirements of Section 4.15 and the following criteria are met.
  50|                        1. Standards: In granting the special permit, the Planning Board shall find that the permit may be granted without
  51|                        substantial detriment to the public good and without nullifying or substantially derogating from the intent and purpose
  52|                        of this bylaw, and shall find that:
  53|                       - the specific site is an appropriate location for such an installation;
  54|                       - the proposed installation will not adversely affect the existing neighborhood;
  55|                       - the granting of the special permit will not reasonably diminish the available light, air, sunlight and other amenities,
  56|                        and;
  57|                                                                                           82

===PAGE 86=== (ocr, 56 lines)
   1|                    - there will be no nuisance or serious hazard to vehicles or pedestrians.
   2|                     2. Conditions: The Planning Board may impose any conditions deemed necessary to achieve the purpose of this Bylaw,
   3|                     such as, but not limited to, the following:
   4|                     - greater than minimum setback requirements;
   5|                     - modification of exterior appearance;
   6|                     - limitation of size or extent of facilities;
   7|                     - regulation of traffic and site plan features;
   8|                    - screening of premises from view by use of appropriate walls, fencing or buffer strips;
   9|                     - control of the number, location, size and lighting of signs;
  10|                    - additional design and siting modifications where appropriate.
  11|                     4.15.4.4 Development Plan Review
  12|                     All Large-Scale Ground-Mounted Solar Photovoltaic Installations shall require Development Plan Review, under Section
  13|                     8.4 of the Zoning Bylaws, by the Lunenburg Planning Board. A public information meeting shall be held by said Board.
  14|                     The Building/Zoning Official shall not issue a building permit unless and until the Planning Board submits an approved
  15|                     Development Plan Review Document, and Special Permit documentation where applicable, to the Building/Zoning
  16|                     Official. A building permit will be issued by the Building/Zoning Official that shows evidence that the project is
  17|                     consistent with state and federal building codes, the findings and directives of the Development Plan Review, and/or
  18|                     Special Permit, and local bylaws and regulations, including those set forth by the Conservation Commission. As-built
  19|                     plans shall be submitted prior to final inspection to the Building /Zoning Official with copies to the Planning Board.
  20|                     4.15.4.5 Utility Notification
  21|                     Evidence shall be provided at time of the application for the Development Plan Review that the utility company that
  22|                     operates the electrical grid where the installation is to be located has been informed of the applicant's intent to
  23|                     construct a solar photovoltaic installation and that approval to connect to the grid has been granted or appropriate
  24|                     application(s) have or will be been made to such utilities for interconnection. Off-grid systems shall be exempt from this
  25|                     requirement. Reasonable efforts should be made to place all utility connections underground, depending on
  26|                     appropriate soil conditions, shape and topography of the site.
  27|                     An application for a Development Plan Review shall be accompanied by the required fee and a tri-party account (in-
  28|                     house escrow account with the Planning Board, Developer and Town Treasurer) for engineering review, monitoring,
  29|                     and inspections fees. An application for a building permit shall be accompanied by the fee required for a building
  30|                     permit. All other fees that shall be required by permitting parties (Conservation Commission, etc.) shall be administered
  31|                     according to their regulations.
  32|                     4.15.4.7 Setbacks and Buffer Strips
  33|                     Buffer Strips in all Districts: All installations shall be surrounded by a buffer strip which shall be two hundred (200) feet
  34|                     in depth in a Residential, Conservation or Recreation District and shall be 50 feet in a Commercial or Industrial district
  35|                     unless it abuts a Residential, Conservation or Recreation District in which case the buffer strip shall be two hundred
  36|                     feet in depth along such abutting lot lines, unless the applicant can demonstrate, and the Planning Board finds, that
  37|                    adequate buffering can be provided in a narrower buffer strip.
  38|                     Setbacks in all Districts:
  39|                     Front Yard: The Front Yard Depth shall be at least 50 feet, provided, however, that when the lot is in a Residential,
  40|                    Outlying or Recreation District or abuts a Residential, Conservation, or a Recreation District, the front lot setback shall
  41|                     not be less than 200 feet.
  42|                     Side Yard: Each side yard setback shall be at least 50 feet, provided, however, that when the lot is in a Residential,
  43|                     Outlying or Recreation District or abuts a Residential, Conservation, or a Recreation District, the side yard setback shall
  44|                     not be less than 200 feet.
  45|                     Rear Yard: The Rear Yard Depth shall be at least 50 feet; provided, when the lot is in a Residential, Outlying or
  46|                     Recreation District or abuts a Residential, Conservation, or a Recreation District, the rear yard setback shall not be less
  47|                    than 200 feet.
  48|                     4.15.4.8 Required Documents
  49|                    In addition to documents required for Development Plan Review, the following will be required for Large-Scale Ground-
  50|                     Mounted Solar Photovoltaic Installation. The Planning Board may waive one of more of these requirements in its sole
  51|                     discretion under appropriate circumstances for Large-Scale Ground-Mounted Solar Photovoltaic Installation between
  52|                    10 kW and 249 kW.
  53|                     A. All plans and maps shall be prepared, stamped and signed by a professional engineer licensed to practice in
  54|                     Massachusetts.
  55|                     B. Property lines and physical features, including roads for property sites.
  56|                                                                                        83

===PAGE 87=== (ocr, 57 lines)
   1|                        C. Proposed changes to landscape of site, including grading, vegetation, clearing, planting, screening vegetation or
   2|                        structures.
   3|                        D. Locations of wetlands and Priority Habitat Areas defined by the Natural Heritage & Endangered Species Program.
   4|                        E. Locations of floodplains or inundation areas for moderate or high hazard dams.
   5|                        F. A list of any hazardous materials proposed to be located on the site in excess of household quantities and a plan to
   6|                        prevent their release to the environment as appropriate. (See Section 4.15.4.9(I))
   7|                        G. Drawings of the installation showing the proposed layout of the system and any potential shading from nearby
   8|                       structures.
   9|                        H. One or three phase line electrical diagrams detailing the installation, associated components and electrical
  10|                       interconnection methods with all National Electrical Code compliant disconnects and overcurrent devices.
  11|                       1. Documentation of the major system components to be used, including the electric generating PV panels, mounting
  12|                       system, inverter, etc.
  13|                       J. Name of property owner, address, telephone number, e-mail
  14|                       K. Name of lessor or lessee, address, telephone number, e-mail
  15|                        L. Name of contact person, address, telephone number, e-mail
  16|                       M. Name of designing engineer, address, telephone number, e-mail
  17|                       N. Names of contractors, address, telephone number, e-mail
  18|                       O. Name of installer, address, telephone number, e-mail
  19|                       P. Zoning District designation for parcel of land, map and parcel.
  20|                       Q. Documentation of actual or prospective access and control of the project site sufficient to allow for construction and
  21|                       operation and maintenance of the proposed solar photovoltaic installation.
  22|                       R. Provision of water including that needed for fire protection.
  23|                       S. Proof of liability insurance.
  24|                       T. Description of the financial surety required by Section 4.15.4.12 below.
  25|                       U. Sight line representations depicting in profile the view of the proposed installation, and any appurtenant structures,
  26|                       from the location upon any public road within 300 feet that would have the most unobstructed view of the installations,
  27|                       and from the closest wall of each residential building within 300 feet of the highest point of the installation.
  28|                       V. A screening plan, in compliance with Section 4.15.4.9(F).
  29|                       4.15.4.9 Design Standards
  30|                       A. Lighting and Security
  31|                       Lighting of solar photovoltaic installations shall be consistent with local, state and federal law. Lighting of other parts
  32|                       of the installation, such as any appurtenant structures, shall be limited to that required for safety and operational
  33|                       purposes and shall be reasonably shielded from abutting properties. Where feasible, any required lighting shall be
  34|                      directed downward and shall incorporate full cut off fixtures to reduce light pollution. Surveillance and security
  35|                       cameras shall be placed as to not infringe on private citizens' property.
  36|                       B. Signage
  37|                       Signage size shall comply with the Zoning Bylaw and shall not be used for displaying any advertising except to identify
  38|                      the owner and/or operator of the solar installation and a 24-hour emergency contact telephone number.
  39|                       C. Land Clearing
  40|                      Clearing of natural vegetation shall be limited to what is necessary for construction, operation and maintenance of the
  41|                      installation Any land disturbance, including earth removal of land cleared greater than one acre shall be subject to
  42|                      Stormwater Management criteria and/or otherwise prescribed by applicable laws, regulations and bylaws.
  43|                       D. Safety, Emergency Service and Environmental Standards
  44|                      The applicant shall provide a copy of the project summary, electrical schematic, and site plan. The applicant shall
  45|                      develop an     emergency response plan including showing all means of shutting down the solar installation. The
  46|                      applicant shall submit the name of the person answerable to inquires throughout the life of the installation. If the
  47|                      designated person changes, the name of the new designated person shall be submitted as an addendum.
  48|                      E. Monitoring and Maintenance
  49|                      The applicant shall submit a plan for the operation and maintenance of the installation which shall include measures for
  50|                      maintaining the site including safe access, stormwater control, structural repairs and the integrity of security measures.
  51|                      These measures must be acceptable to the Fire Chief and emergency medical services personnel. If needed, training of
  52|                      service personnel will be provided by the applicant. The owner/operator shall be responsible for the cost of maintaining
  53|                      the installation.
  54|                      The applicant shall also submit a Monitoring/Inspection Form under the Development Plan Review during construction,
  55|                      and shall further submit a report on the condition of the structure and site by January 15th of each year.
  56|                      F. Visual Impact
  57|                                                                                          84

===PAGE 88=== (ocr, 31 lines)
   1|                        Any Large-Scale Ground-Mounted Solar Photovoltaic Installation shall be designed to minimize visual impacts
   2|                       including preserving natural vegetation to the maximum extent possible, blending in equipment with the surroundings, and adding vegetative buffers to screen abutting residential properties whether developed or not. Siting shall be such
   3|                       that the view of the solar electric generating installation from other areas of Town shall be as minimal as possible. Buffer Strips shall surround the proposed project. A screening plan, that assures the facility is shielded to greatest
   4|                       G. Height extent possible from public view, shall be required to be reviewed under the Development Plan Review.

   6|                       H. Roads

   8|                      I. Hazardous Materials

  14|                      Environmental Protection's Division of Air Quality noise regulations, 310CMR 7.10. The site shall not produce any other vibration, harmonics, or interference, which would be perceived or impact the normal function of electronics off site.
